Shadows are Bicategorical Traces
Kathryn Hess and Nima Rasekh

Abstract
. The theory of shadows is an axiomatic, bicategorical framework that generalizes topological Hochschild homology (THH) and satisfies analogous important properties, such as Morita invariance. Using Berman’s extension of THH \mathrm{THH} to bicategories, we prove that there is an equivalence between functors out of Hochschild homology of a bicategory and shadows on that bicategory. As part of that proof we give a computational description of pseudo-functors out of the truncated simplex category and a variety thereof, which can be of independent interest. Building on this result we present three applications: (1) We provide a new, conceptual proof that shadows, and as a result the Euler characteristic and trace introduced by Campbell and Ponto, are Morita invariant. (2) We strengthen this result by using an explicit computation of the Hochschild homology of the free adjunction bicategory to show that the construction of the Euler characteristic is homotopically unique. (3) We generalize the construction of 𝒱 \mathscr{V} -enriched Hochschild homology, where 𝒱 \mathscr{V} is a presentably symmetric monoidal ∞ \infty -category, to bimodules, and prove it gives us a shadow.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
From THH to shadows

Hochschild homology of associative rings, first studied by Hochschild
[
Hoc45
]
and Cartan-Eilenberg
[
CE56
]
, generalizes the notion of Kähler differentials from the commutative to the merely associative setting. Hochschild homology is an important tool for non-commutative geometry
[
CM08
]
, which satisfies interesting properties such as Morita invariance
[
Lod98
]
and which provides a useful approximation to algebraic
K
-theory via the Dennis trace
[
Den76
,
Wal79
]
. The homotopical analogue of classical Hochschild homology, topological Hochschild homology (
\mathrm{THH}
) of ring spectra, admits an analogous Dennis trace map from algebraic
K
-theory
[
Bök85
,
EKMM97
]
.

Since the definition of algebraic
K
-theory has been extended to many types of structured categories
[
Wal85
]
, the important connection between
\mathrm{THH}
and algebraic
K
-theory of ring spectra inspired the definition of similar extensions of (topological) Hochschild homology: to exact categories
[
McC94
]
, to dg-categories
[
Kel99
]
, and to spectral categories
[
BM12
]
, as a special case of topological Hochschild homology of bimodules over spectral categories. It can be be challenging to establish properties, such as Morita invariance, of certain of these versions of THH.

An axiomatic framework that is able to incorporate simultaneously all of these different settings is therefore worth developing. Ponto provided one potential such framework, when she introduced the notion of a
shadow of bicategories
, as tool to study fixed point phenomena
[
Pon10
]
. In
[
CP19
]
she and Campbell proved that topological Hochschild homology of bimodules over spectral categories indeed provides an example of a shadow. They showed moreover that Morita invariance of
\mathrm{THH}
of ring spectra is simply a special case of a more general, abstract Morita invariance of shadows. This observation, combined with the possibility of performing concrete computations with shadows via string diagrams
[
PS13
]
, provides strong incentive to determine whether the extensions of
\mathrm{THH}
to other types of structured categories are also shadows, and, if so, how homotopy coherent they are. The issue of homotopy coherence is the focus of our attention in this article.

1.2.
The rise of homotopy coherence

Over the past two decades, a rich theory of
\infty
-categories has been developed
[
Joy08a
,
Joy08b
,
Lur09
]
, in which, instead of focusing only on homotopy categories, one studies objects of interest in a homotopy-coherent fashion. The focus on
\infty
-categories has had a significant influence on the study of algebraic
K
-theory and
\mathrm{THH}
. In this framework, algebraic
K
-theory can be described as a functor associating spaces to stable
\infty
-categories of a certain type, which satisfies certain universal properties, giving rise to a new definition of the Dennis trace to
\mathrm{THH}

[
BGT13
,
BGT14
,
BGMN21
]
. The
\infty
-categorical perspective provides new insight into
\mathrm{THH}
of ring spectra and its cyclotomic structure
[
NS18
,
NS19
]
.

As sketched above, the development of various notions of
\mathrm{THH}
eventually inspired the notion of a shadow on a bicategory, which captures the essential structure of those diverse constructions. Given that many of these construction have now been generalized to the
\infty
-categorical framework, it is natural to seek an analogous definition of an
\infty
-categorical shadow. Since shadows were originally defined on bicategories, the
\infty
-categorical analogue should be defined on
(\infty,2)
-categories, which are the higher categorical analogue of bicategories
[
Ber20
]
.

The existence of shadows on
(\infty,2)
-categories would have interesting practical consequences as well. For example, there is a natural definition of coHochschild homology (
\mathrm{co}\mathrm{THH}
) of coalgebra spectra
[
HS21
,
BGH+18
,
BGS21
]
, dual to that of
\mathrm{THH}
. Given this duality, there should be an axiomatic, shadow-type approach to
\mathrm{co}\mathrm{THH}
. However, since essentially the only examples of coalgebras in point-set models of spectra are suspension spectra
[
PS19
]
, a strict bicategorical approach to studying
\mathrm{co}\mathrm{THH}
would be of limited interest. An
(\infty,2)
-categorical approach is necessary, and elaborating and analyzing an
(\infty,2)
-categorical version of shadows is an important first step in this direction.

1.3.
From shadows back to THH

To determine how best to generalize shadows from bicategories to
(\infty,2)
-categories, it is helpful to examine more carefully the relationship between shadows and
\mathrm{THH}
. In particular, given that shadows are defined in the abstract setting of bicategories, how is it that they satisfy Morita invariance, which is often thought of as a concrete property of rings and
\mathrm{THH}
?

The goal of this work is to tackle this question, exploiting the construction of
\mathrm{THH}
of enriched
\infty
-categories introduced by Berman
[
Ber22
]
, in the case of enrichments in the
\infty
-category of categories. Berman formulated an efficient definition of Hochschild homology of enriched
\infty
-categories in terms of the colimit of a certain cyclic bar construction taking values in the enriching
\infty
-category, avoiding the sophisticated machinery of enriched
\infty
-categories due to Gepner and Haugseng
[
GH15
]
. Applying Berman’s construction to the
\infty
-category of categories, we prove following result relating Hochschild Homology of bicategories and shadows.

Theorem
3.19
.

For any bicategory
\mathscr{B}
and category
\mathscr{D}
, there is a natural equivalence of categories

\mathrm{Fun}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B}),\mathscr{D}\big)\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D}).

Here
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
, which is a category, is Hochschild homology of a bicategory
\mathscr{B}
(
2.10
),
\mathrm{Fun}(-,-)
denotes the functor category, and
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}
is the category of shadows on
\mathscr{B}
taking values in
\mathscr{D}
(
3.17
).

This result has many interesting implications of a philosophical, as well as mathematical, nature.

Why shadows resemble THH:
Our main theorem enables us to explain why shadows satisfy many properties that also hold for THH, since it implies that the axioms of a shadow are precisely the conditions required to construct a cocone out of the diagram that defines
\mathrm{THH}
. Working in an
\infty
-categorical framework, rather than in the context of model categories, facilitated making this connections, since (co)limits of diagrams of
\infty
-categories make natural sense
[
Lur09
]
.

Tricategorical Shadows:
A key step in the proof of
3.19
is to characterize precisely pseudo-categorical
2
-truncated simplicial cocones (cf.
Appendix

A
) and then to relate this characterization to shadows. This opens up the possibility of defining tricategorical shadows by a similar method, via a characterization of
3
-truncated simplicial cocones.

Morita invariance:
Our reformulation of shadow functors out of
\mathrm{THH}
of bicategories allows us to give a new proof of the Morita invariance of shadows, by lifting both the “Euler characteristic” construction and the “trace construction” of Campbell and Ponto
[
CP19
]
to a functor (
4.9
/
4.16
), providing insight into the essence of this fundamental property. The proofs of these statement rely on having a basic understanding of the structure of Hochschild homology of the free adjunction category and a variety thereof. Beyond those implications, this new proof thus strongly hints at further generalizations of Morita invariance to other (enriched) settings.

Homotopy canonical Euler characteristic and trace:
Reformulating Morita invariance using certain structures of the Hochschild homology of the free adjunction enables us to make a much deeper analysis of the Euler characteristic and trace. Concretely, by explicitly computing the whole Hochschild homology of the free adjunction
2
-category (
5.23
) and a generalization thereof (
5.26
), we deduce that the definition of the Euler characteristic is canonical (
5.25
), whereas the definition of trace depends on a choice of automorphism (
5.28
). Our result provides another example of how a computation related to the free adjunction bicategory leads to formal results, similar to recent work of Ayala and Francis
[
AF21
]
1
1

1

Using an alternative approach to
\mathrm{THH}
of enriched categories via factorization homology
[
AFR17
]
.

Property vs. computation:
A key part of the previous items was to translate a desirable property regarding
\mathrm{THH}
, such as Morita invariance, into computing
\mathrm{THH}
of the representing
2
-category, in this case the free adjunction
2
-category. This should be seen as one example of a general method we introduce that translates properties of
\mathrm{THH}
into
\mathrm{THH}
computations and is expected to have many further applications.

Shadows of
(\infty,2)
-categories and
\mathrm{THH}
of
\infty
-bimodules:
Knowing that shadows on a bicategory
\mathscr{B}
are equivalent to functors out of the category
\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{B})
enables us to formulate a reasonable
(\infty,2)
-categorical generalization that coincides appropriately with the classical definition upon restriction to the homotopy bicategory. As a first application we define
\mathrm{THH}
of enriched
\infty
-categorical bimodules in the sense of Haugseng
[
Hau16
]
and prove it lifts to a functor

\widehat{\mathrm{THH}}:\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}})\to\mathrm{Ho}\mathscr{V}
(
6.11
).

1.4.
Where do we go from here?

The results proven here suggest several interesting and natural next steps.

(1)

\mathrm{co}\mathrm{THH}
via shadows:
As discussed above, we cannot expect to study
\mathrm{co}\mathrm{THH}
at a point-set level. The results here create an opportunity to define
\mathrm{co}\mathrm{THH}
of a coalgebra spectrum as
\mathrm{THH}
of its category of comodules.

(2)

Functoriality of
\mathrm{THH}
:
One current challenge when studying
\mathrm{THH}
of enriched
\infty
-categories is that the definition given in
[
Ber22
]
is not known to be functorial. Having a working functorial construction would, for example, permit us to generalize the Morita invariance of shadows beyond bicategories.

(3)

\mathrm{THH}
is a trace:
A key result in
[
CP19
]
is that
\mathrm{THH}
for bimodules over spectral categories itself is a shadow. In
6.11
we have generalized this result to a functor of
\infty
-categories valued in
\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{V})
, however, the expectation is that it should lift to an
\infty
-categorical functor

\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{V})\to\mathscr{V},

which should, under the right circumstances, even be
\mathscr{V}
-enriched, as has been discussed in
6.2
, if some theoretical challenges regarding enriched
(\infty,2)
-categories can be resolved (
6.14
).

1.5.
Organization

The paper is organized as follows. We begin by reviewing in the first section relevant content about shadows and
\mathrm{THH}
of enriched
\infty
-categories. In the second section, we compute
\mathrm{THH}
of bicategories and use the result to prove that traces of bicategories are equivalent to shadows. Based on this characterization, we provide our new proof for Morita invariance of shadows on bicategories in the third section. Finally, in the last section, we deduce from the equivalence between shadows and
\mathrm{THH}
that it is reasonable to define a homotopy-coherent shadow to be a trace on an
(\infty,2)
-category.

1.6.
Notation

We use several types of categories throughout this article and thus need to distinguish between them carefully.

A
category
is a
(1,1)
-category. We denote by
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
the large
(2,1)
-category of small categories, i.e.,
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
has

•

small categories as objects,

•

functors as morphisms, and

•

natural isomorphisms as
2
-morphisms.

In particular, the existence of a commuting diagram of functors
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{3}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}_{4}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{4}}

\scriptstyle\cong

means that there is a natural isomorphism

\alpha:F_{3}\circ F_{1}\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }F_{4}\circ F_{2}.

For any two categories
\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}
, we let
\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
denote the
functor category
, whose objects are functors from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{D}
, and whose morphisms are natural transformations.

In this article a
bicategory

\mathscr{B}
is a
(2,2)
-category, composed of

•

a class of objects
X,Y,Z,...
,

•

a category of morphisms
\mathscr{B}(X,Y)
for every pair of objects
X,Y
, where the unit object in
\mathscr{B}(X,X)
is denoted
U_{X}
, and

•

natural isomorphisms
a
,
r
and
l
that witness associativity, right unitality, and left unitality, respectively and that satisfy certain axioms that the reader can find, for example, in
[
Bén67
,
Lei98
]
.

Note that
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
is an example of a bicategory.

In what follows, the terminology
\infty
-category
is used as a synonym for
quasi-category
, one important model of
(\infty,1)
-categories, popularized by Lurie
[
Lur09
]
. We denote the large
\infty
-category of small
\infty
-categories by
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. The nerve functor
N
from the category
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
of small categories to that of simplicial sets enables us to see
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
as a subcategory of
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
. We routinely suppress the functor
N
to simplify notation. The inclusion of
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
into
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}
admits a left adjoint

\mathrm{Ho}:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}

that takes each
\infty
-category to its
homotopy category
.

If
\mathscr{C}
is an
\infty
-category, we let
\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{C})
denote the
\infty
-category of space-valued presheaves on
\mathscr{C}
.

Though we also use
(\infty,2)
-categories in this article, we assume no prior knowledge of them. All the necessary theory of
(\infty,2)
-categories is reviewed in
Section

2.2
.

We work as well with various notions of the “category of spectra”. In the first part of
Section

2.1
, we consider a monoidal model category of spectra, denoted
\mathcal{S}p
, e.g., symmetric spectra or orthogonal spectra
[
MMSS01
]
. We denote the monoidal product on
\mathcal{S}p
by
\wedge
. We let
\mathrm{Sp}
denote the
\infty
-category of spectra and
\mathrm{Ho}(\mathrm{Sp})
its homotopy category, which is equivalent to the homotopy category of the model category
\mathcal{S}p
.

1.7.
Background

We assume only basic familiarity with category theory and a healthy curiosity about
\mathrm{THH}
.
We review in
Section

2
the relevant definitions of
\mathrm{THH}
of spectral categories, shadows, enriched
\infty
-categories, and the
\infty
-categorical definition of
\mathrm{THH}
of enriched
\infty
-categories.

Acknowledgments

We would like to thank John Berman for helpful conversations and for clarifying several points in his paper
[
Ber22
]
. We also like to thank Rune Haugseng for helpful discussions regarding his paper
[
Hau16

. Finally, we would like to thank Thomas Nikolaus for helpful discussions and suggestions.

2.
Background

In this section we recall relevant definitions in two (seemingly distinct) areas: shadows of bicategories and

\mathrm{THH}
of enriched
\infty
-categories. The first subsection is written at the point-set level, in the language of bicategories and model categories. The material presented here can be found in more detail in
[
CP19
]
.
The second subsection, based on
[
Ber22
]
, reviews the relevant concepts about enriched
\infty
-categories and their topological Hochschild homology, which is not defined on the point-set level.

2.1.
Shadows of bicategories

We review here the notion of a shadow, which can be viewed as an axiomatization of
\mathrm{THH}
. Shadows first appeared in
[
Pon10
]
, though
[
CP19
]
is our main reference. Topological Hochschild homology (
\mathrm{THH}
) was originally defined for ring spectra
[
Bök85
]
, but was then generalized to spectral categories, i.e., categories enriched over a monoidal model category of spectra
\mathcal{S}p

[
BM12
]
.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{D}
be spectral categories.

(1)

A
\mathscr{C}
-module
is a spectral functor

\mathscr{C}\to\mathcal{S}p.

(2)

A
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
-bimodule
is a spectral functor

\mathscr{C}^{op}\wedge\mathscr{D}\to\mathcal{S}p,

where
\mathscr{C}^{op}\wedge\mathscr{D}
is the spectral category with as objects ordered pairs
(c,d)
, where
c\in\mathscr{C}
and
d\in\mathscr{D}
, and with mapping spectrum

\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}^{op}\wedge\mathscr{D}}\big((c_{1},d_{1}),(c_{2},d_{2})\big)=\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{C}}(c_{1},c_{2})\wedge\mathrm{Map}_{\mathscr{D}}(d_{1},d_{2}).

Definition 2.2

(
[
CP19
, Definition 2.5]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a be a pointwise-cofibrant spectral category (i.e.,
\mathscr{C}(c,d)
is a cofibrant spectrum for all objects
c,d
) and
\mathscr{D}
a
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
-bimodule that is pointwise-cofibrant as a spectral category. The
topological Hochschild homology of
\mathscr{C}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{D}
, denoted
\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C};\mathscr{D})
, is the geometric realization of the cyclic bar construction
N^{cy}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
, i.e., the simplicial spectrum that at level
n
is

N^{cy}_{n}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})=\bigvee_{c_{0},...,c_{n}}\mathscr{C}(c_{0},c_{1})\wedge\mathscr{C}(c_{1},c_{2})\wedge...\wedge\mathscr{D}(c_{n},c_{0}),

with faces given by composition in
\mathscr{C}
or the action of
\mathscr{C}
on
\mathscr{D}
(together with a cyclic permutation for the last face) and degeneracies given by the unit,
i.e.,

\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C};\mathscr{D})=|N^{cy}_{\bullet}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})|.

Ponto’s key insight was that this definition could be axiomatized. Concretely, let
\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od})
be the bicategory with pointwise-cofibrant small spectral categories as objects and with the morphism category from
\mathscr{C}
to
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
equal to the homotopy category of
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
-bimodules,
\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}^{\prime})})
.
For every pointwise-cofibrant spectral category
\mathscr{C}
, topological Hochschild homology gives rise to a collection of functors

\mathrm{THH}:\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od})(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})=\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})})\to\mathrm{Ho}(\mathrm{Sp}):\mathscr{D}\mapsto\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C};\mathscr{D}),

satisfying certain properties, which become the axioms in the definition of a shadow, which we recall now.

Definition 2.3

(
[
CP19
, Definition 2.16]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a bicategory with associator
a
, left unit
l
, and right unit
r
, and let
\mathscr{D}
be a category.
A
shadow
on
\mathscr{B}
with values in
\mathscr{D}
is a functor

\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle:\coprod_{X\in\mathrm{Obj}(\mathscr{B})}\mathscr{B}(X,X)\to\mathscr{D}

that satisfies the following conditions.

For every pair of 1-morphisms
F:X\to Y
and
G:Y\to X
in
\mathscr{B}
that are composable in either order, there is a natural isomorphism

\theta:\langle\langle FG\rangle\rangle\xrightarrow{\ \cong\ }\langle\langle GF\rangle\rangle

such that for all
F:X\to Y
,
G:Y\to Z
,
H:Z\to X
, and
K:X\to X
the following diagrams in
\mathscr{D}
commute up to natural isomorphism.

(2.4)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle H(GF)\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle(GF)H\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle G(FH)\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle(HG)F\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle F(HG)\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle(FH)G\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

(2.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle KU_{X}\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle U_{X}K\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle KU_{X}\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle K\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle r\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle l\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle r\rangle\rangle}

The primary example of a shadow is
\mathrm{THH}
of spectral categories.

Theorem 2.6

(
[
CP19
, Theorem 2.17]
)
.

Topological Hochschild homology of spectral categories is a shadow, i.e., the family of functors

\Big\{\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C};-):\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})})\to\mathrm{Ho}(\mathrm{Sp}):\mathscr{D}\mapsto\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C};\mathscr{D})\mid\mathscr{C}\in\mathrm{Ob}\,\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od})\Big\}

is such that for all pointwise-cofibrant spectral categories
\mathscr{C}
and
\mathscr{C}^{\prime}
,
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C}^{\prime})
-bimodules
\mathscr{D}
, and
(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathscr{C})
-bimodules
\mathscr{D}^{\prime}
, there is a natural isomorphism

\theta:\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D}\wedge_{\mathscr{C}^{\prime}}\mathscr{D}^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C}^{\prime},\mathscr{D}^{\prime}\wedge_{\mathscr{C}}\mathscr{D})

satisfying the conditions of
2.3
.

It is common to simplify notation and to write

\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C})=\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C};\mathscr{C}),

where we consider
\mathscr{C}
as a bimodule over itself in the obvious way. Note that computing THH of a spectral category yields a spectrum, i.e., an object in the enriching category.

The axioms of a shadow suffice to prove interesting properties that are known to hold for
\mathrm{THH}
. For example, Ponto and Campbell prove a general form of Morita equivalence for shadows
[
CP19
, Proposition 4.6, Proposition 4.8]
that implies the classical Morita equivalence for ring spectra
[
CP19
, Example 5.10]
.

In
3.19
we show that these properties stem from a deep connection between shadows and
\mathrm{THH}
. However, in order to understand this relation we need a higher categorical approach to shadows that moves away from point-set models. Laying the foundations of such an approach is the goal of the next subsection.

2.2.
\mathrm{THH}
of enriched
\infty
-categories

In this subsection we review the definition of
\mathrm{THH}
of an enriched
\infty
-category.
Before we move on to the enriched setting, it is valuable to explain why we cannot simply generalize
2.2
.

An
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
is a simplicial set satisfying additional conditions, the so-called “inner horn lifting conditions”
[
Lur09
]
. These lifting conditions imply that for any two objects (i.e.,
0
-simplices)
x
and
y
, the simplicial set
\mathrm{Map}(x,y)
given by the fiber of the map
\mathscr{C}^{\Delta^{1}}\to\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C}
over the point
(x,y)
is a Kan complex and thus a homotopy-meaningful mapping space. There is no direct composition map, however. Instead, for three objects
x,y,z
, there is a zig-zag

\mathrm{Map}(x,y)\times\mathrm{Map}(y,z)\xleftarrow{\simeq}\mathrm{Map}(x,y,z)\to\mathrm{Map}(x,z)

where
\mathrm{Map}(x,y,z)
is the fiber of the map
\mathscr{C}^{\Delta^{2}}\to\mathscr{C}^{3}
over
(x,y,z)
, and the lifting conditions imply that the first map is a trivial Kan fibration.

This observation has two unfortunate implications.

(1)

We cannot just define an enriched
\infty
-category in terms of the existence of composition maps and thus need a more complicated notion of enrichment.

(2)

We cannot just define
\mathrm{THH}
via the cyclic bar construction, as the simplicial structure relies on the existence of a direct composition map.

Fortunately, Gepner-Haugseng
[
GH15
]
and Hinich
[
Hin18
]
have developed notions of
\infty
-categories enriched in a monoidal
\infty
-category, both of which are based on an operadic approach to classical enriched category theory, generalized to the
\infty
-categorical setting. Their constructions are very powerful and have been used to prove deep results about enriched
\infty
-categories, but are also very intricate and can be difficult to use for computations.

As in this article we need a notion of enrichment only to study
\mathrm{THH}
, we can focus on the case where the enrichment category is not just monoidal, but actually
symmetric monoidal
.
There is a much more convenient way of defining enriched
\infty
-categories in the symmetric case, due to Berman
[
Ber22
]
2
2

2

This approach can be in fact generalized to encompass the monoidal case, however, we do not require this generality.
, which also provides a natural framework for a generalization of
\mathrm{THH}
.

The key idea is that of a
bypass operation
, which we define next. Recall that a
directed multigraph
is a pair of sets
\Gamma=(S,E)
of vertices and of directed edges between them. For two multigraphs
\Gamma_{1},\Gamma_{2}
with the same set of vertices
S
, there is a
bypass operation
from
\Gamma_{1}
to
\Gamma_{2}
if
\Gamma_{2}
can be obtained from
\Gamma_{1}
by a sequence of the following combinatorial moves.

(1)

Adding a loop

(2)

Replacing a path of consecutive edges
e_{1},...,e_{n}
by a single edge
e
.

For a precise definition, see
[
Ber22
, Definition 2.1]
.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
S
be any set. The
bypass category on
S
,
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}
, has as objects all directed multigraphs with fixed vertex set
S
and a finite set of edges and as morphisms all bypass operations between such.

Note that
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}
admits a symmetric monoidal product
\otimes
, where the set of edges of
\Gamma_{1}\otimes\Gamma_{2}
is the disjoint union of the sets of edges of
\Gamma_{1}
and
\Gamma_{2}
, and the unit is the multigraph with no edges.

Definition 2.8
.

Let
\mathscr{V}
be a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category. A
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category with set of objects
S
is a symmetric monoidal functor of
\infty
-categories

\mathscr{C}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}\to\mathscr{V}.

The bypass category is designed to encode the structure of an enriched category. For example, if
(X_{0},...,X_{n})
denotes the multigraph with
n+1
vertices
X_{0},...,X_{n}
and a unique edge from
X_{i}
to
X_{i+1}
for every
i
, then

\mathscr{C}(X_{0},...,X_{n})\in\mathscr{V}

can be viewed as the object in
\mathscr{V}
of sequences of
n
composable maps

X_{0}\to...\to X_{n}
. In particular
\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})
plays the role of the mapping object. We can then use a bypass operation of type (2) above to get the desired composition map

\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2})\to\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{2}).

The following result should thus not be very surprising.

Proposition 2.9

(
[
Ber22
, Proposition 2.7]
)
.

If
\mathscr{V}
is a symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category, then the definition of
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category given here agrees with that in
[
GH15
]
.

We can use this simplified definition of an enriched
\infty
-category (and in particular the fact that
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}
is a category) to define
\mathrm{THH}
of a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category as follows. Let
\Lambda
be the category with finite cyclically ordered sets
0<1<...<n<0
, for
n\geq 0
, as objects and maps that respect the cyclic structure as morphisms. (For more details see
[
Ber22
, Definition 3.3]
.) Let

(\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}})_{\bullet}:\Lambda\to\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S})

be the functor that takes a cyclic set
0<1<...<n<0
to the coproduct of representables

\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S}(X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n},X_{0}),

and let

\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}}=\mathrm{colim}_{{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}}(\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}})_{\bullet}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}).

We suppress the choice of the set
S
of objects from the notation
(\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}})_{\bullet}
and
\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}}
.

As Berman explains, any enriched
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}\to\mathscr{V}
extends to a colimit-preserving functor

\mathscr{C}_{*}:\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S})\to\mathscr{V},

which allows us to formulate the following generalization of
\mathrm{THH}
, providing a way of associating an object in
\mathscr{V}
to any
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category.

Definition 2.10

(
[
Ber22
, Definition 4.1]
)
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category. The
\mathscr{V}
-enriched Hochschild homology
of
\mathscr{C}
is

\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C})=\mathscr{C}_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}})\in\mathrm{Obj}\mathscr{V}.

Remark 2.11
.

Notice,
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}
is simply denoted by
\mathrm{THH}
in
[
Ber22
]
, however, as we will analyze Hochschild homology with several different enrichments, we have chosen a notation that makes the enrichment explicit.

Tracing through the definitions, since
\mathscr{C}_{*}
preserves colimits, we see that
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C})
is the colimit of a cyclic diagram that at level
n
is equivalent to

\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S}\mathscr{C}(X_{0},...,X_{n},X_{0})\simeq\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}}\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{n},X_{0})

because
\mathscr{C}
is symmetric monoidal,

Example 2.12
.

If
\mathscr{V}=(\mathrm{Sp},\wedge)
, the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of spectra with the smash product, then a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
is a spectral
\infty
-category, and
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathrm{Sp}}(\mathscr{C})
is the
\infty
-categorical analogue of
2.2
, and we will hence simply denote it by
\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C})
.

Example 2.13
.

If
\mathscr{V}=(\mathscr{S},\times)
, the symmetric monoidal
\infty
-category of spaces, then
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories are precisely
non-enriched
\infty
-categories. More concretely, we get complete Segal spaces
[
Rez01
]
, as follows from
[
GH15
, Theorem 4.4.7, Remark 5.3.10]
. We hence denote
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{S}}
by
\mathrm{HH}_{\infty}
.

Example 2.14
.

If
\mathscr{V}=(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at},\times)
, then we call a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
a
bicategory
. Note that if
\mathscr{C}
is an bicategory, then
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}}(\mathscr{C})
is a category, and we hence use the notation
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{C})
.

Example 2.15
.

If
\mathscr{V}=(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty},\times)
, then we call a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
an
(\infty,2)
-category
. Note that if
\mathscr{C}
is an
(\infty,2)
-category, then
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}}(\mathscr{C})
is an
\infty
-category, and we hence use the notation
\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{C})
.

Remark 2.16
.

There are many models of
(\infty,2)
-categories in the literature, such as
2
-complicial sets
[
Ver08
]
,
2
-fold complete Segal spaces
[
Bar05
]
, and
\Theta_{2}
-spaces
[
Rez10
]
.
The model of
(\infty,2)
-categories constructed in
2.15
corresponds to
2
-fold complete Segal spaces, as follows from the proof in
[
GH15
, Theorem 4.4.7, Remark 5.3.10]
, with the
\infty
-category of complete Segal spaces
css
replacing the
\infty
-category
\mathscr{S}
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category with set of objects
S
, and let
\tau:\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C})\to V
be a morphism in
\mathscr{V}
. From
\tau
we can derive a family of morphisms in
\mathscr{V}

\{\tau_{X}:\mathscr{C}(X,X)\to V\mid X\in S\}.

The cyclic structure of
(\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}})_{\bullet}
implies that

\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{1},X_{0})\simeq\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{0})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{X_{0}}}
\textstyle{V}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(X_{1},X_{0})\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\simeq\mathscr{C}(X_{1},X_{0},X_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}(X_{1},X_{1})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tau_{X_{1}}}

commutes for all
X_{0},X_{1}\in S
, where the horizontal “composition” maps arise from bypasses, and the lefthand vertical arrow is given by the cyclic structure. In other words, the value of
\tau
on a composite of two 1-cells that can be composed in either order is independent of the order of composition, just as the trace of a product of two matrices that can be multiplied in either order is independent of the order of multiplication. This observation justifies the terminology in the next definition.

Definition 2.17
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category, and let
V
be an object in
\mathscr{V}
. A
trace
out of
\mathscr{C}
into
V
is a morphism
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C})\to V
in
\mathscr{V}
.

3.
Shadows vs. Traces

In this section we compare the point-set and
\infty
-categorical approaches to Hochschild homology and deduce an equivalence between shadows on a bicategory
\mathscr{B}
with values in a category
\mathscr{D}
and traces out of
\mathscr{B}
into
\mathscr{D}
. To make sense of this comparison, we need in particular to explain how to define a category that can reasonably be called the Hochschild homology of
\mathscr{B}
.

Remark 3.1
.

Motivated by the analysis in
Section

2.2
and in order to simplify notation, for a given
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
, we abuse notation and write

\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n},X_{0})=\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{1},X_{2})...\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{n},X_{0}).

3.1.
Hochschild homology of bicategories

Since bicategories are
(2,2)
-categories, i.e., categories enriched in categories
[
GH15
, Definition 6.1.1]
, it follows from
[
Ber22
, Proposition 2.7]
that a bicategory is a
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
-enriched category in the sense of
2.8
, where
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
is equipped with its Cartesian symmetric monoidal structure. We can therefore apply
2.10
to associate to any bicategory
\mathscr{B}
a category
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
, which is its topological Hochschild homology.

The following, more explicit description of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
proves useful in the next subsection.

Proposition 3.2
.

For every bicategory
\mathscr{B}
, the category
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
is equivalent to the colimit of

(3.3)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

in
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
.

Proof.

Since
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
is a
(2,1)
-category, for any simplicial diagram
F:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
, there is an equivalence

\mathrm{colim}({\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\xrightarrow{F}\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})\simeq\mathrm{colim}(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\xrightarrow{i^{op}}{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\xrightarrow{F}\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})

where
i:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2}\to{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
is the natural inclusion map of the full subcategory with objects
[0],[1]
and
[2]

[
Lur17
, Lemma 1.3.3.10]
.
∎

Remark 3.4
.

While it is not yet proven in full generality that the construction of
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}
(
2.10
) is functorial, the explicit formula in
Equation

3.3
implies that when
\mathscr{V}=(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at},\times)
, the construction of
\mathrm{biHH}
is indeed functorial.

Remark 3.5
.

In order to define
\mathrm{biHH}
of a bicategory, none of the definitions in
Section

2.2
is actually needed: we could simply have defined it to be the colimit of
2.10

However, in
Section

6
, when we compare
\mathrm{biHH}
of bicategories and
\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}
of
(\infty,2)
-categories, the more abstract description proves necessary.

Notation 3.6
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a bicategory. For objects
B_{1},B_{2}
chosen among

\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0}),\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0}),\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0})

we use
\mathrm{Fun}^{\Delta}(B_{1},B_{2})
to denote the subcategory of
\mathrm{Fun}(B_{1},B_{2})
consisting of functors and natural isomorphisms generated by those in
Equation

3.3
.

Part of the data of a bicategory are explicit composition maps, which one can view as giving rise to the face maps
d_{i}
in
2.10
. In particular, again abusing notation somewhat,
d_{0},d_{1}:\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})\to\mathscr{B}(X_{i},X_{i})
(where
i=0,1
) are given by

d_{0}(F,G)=FG,\ d_{1}(F,G)=GF.

Moreover, the face maps on
\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0})
are given by

d_{0}(F,G,H)=(FG,H),\ d_{1}(F,G,H)=(F,GH),\ d_{2}(F,G,H)=(HF,G)

This explicit structure plays an important role in later sections.

It is important to note that the colimit of
Equation

3.3
is evaluated in the
(2,1)
-category
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
, which differs from the
1
-categorical colimit in the category of categories, but rather corresponds to the
pseudocolimit

[
Kel89
]
. Computing general pseudocolimits can be quite challenging, although there certain helpful methods via weighted colimits of enriched categories
[
Kel05
]
and homotopy colimits of model categories
[
Gam08
]
. The example below illustrates the challenges that arise even in simple examples.

Example 3.7
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\otimes,I)
be a monoidal category, and let
B\mathscr{C}
be the bicategory with one object and morphism category given by
\mathscr{C}
, with composition given by the monoidal product. Interpreting
Equation

3.3
in this particular case, we see that
\mathrm{biHH}(B\mathscr{C})
is the colimit of the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C}\times\mathscr{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}

.

Although this diagram is easy to describe explicitly, there is no easy way to compute its colimit. In general the only description available of the category
\mathrm{biHH}(B\mathscr{C})
is formulated in terms of generators and relations
[
ML98
, Section II.8]
, which is generally not useful for computations.

In certain cases we can give a more explicit description of
\mathrm{biHH}(B\mathscr{C})
, which is the content of
Section

5
, resulting in
5.15
.

One case in which we can more easily compute
\mathrm{biHH}
of a bicategory, using homotopy theory, arises when the bicategory is actually a
(2,0)
-category, i.e., all
1
-cells and
2
-cells are equivalences. We call such a bicategory a
bigroupoid
and denote the (3,1)-category of bigroupoids by
\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}_{2}
. To compute
\mathrm{biHH}
of a bigroupoid, we need to understand the relationship between bigroupoids and spaces.

Remark 3.8
.

Let
\mathscr{S}
be the
\infty
-category of spaces. By
[
GH15
, Corollary 6.1.10]
, there is an adjunction of
\infty
-categories

(3.9)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{\leq 1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}

where
\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}
denotes the
(2,1)
-category of groupoids,
of which the right adjoint is fully faithful, with essential image given by
1
-truncated spaces (spaces with trivial homotopy groups above degree
1
). The right adjoint takes a groupoid
\mathscr{G}
to a Kan complex of which the
0
-cells are the objects of
\mathscr{G}
, while for any two objects
X,Y
, there is an isomorphism of sets
\mathrm{Map}_{N\mathscr{G}}(X,Y)\cong\mathscr{G}(X,Y)
, where
\mathrm{Map}_{N\mathscr{G}}
denotes the path space.

The same corollary (
[
GH15
, Corollary 6.1.10]
) implies that there is an adjunction

(3.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Pi_{\leq 2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{2}}

of which the right adjoint is fully faithful, with essential image given by
2
-truncated spaces. By
[
GH15
, Lemma 6.1.9]
the functor
N_{2}
takes a bigroupoid
\mathscr{G}
to a Kan complex
N_{2}\mathscr{G}
, of which the
0
-cells are the objects of
\mathscr{G}
, while for any two objects
X,Y
, there is an equivalence of groupoids

\Pi_{1}\mathrm{Map}_{N_{2}\mathscr{G}}(X,Y)\simeq\mathscr{G}(X,Y).

We can use the adjunction above to compute
\mathrm{THH}
of a bigroupoid.

Example 3.11
.

For any bigroupoid
\mathscr{G}
, there is an equivalence

\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{G})=|\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}}\mathscr{G}(X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{0})|\simeq|\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}}\Pi_{1}\mathrm{Map}_{N_{2}\mathscr{G}}(X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{0})|

\simeq\Pi_{1}|\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}}\mathrm{Map}_{N_{2}\mathscr{G}}(X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{0})|

where
\mathrm{Map}_{N_{2}\mathscr{G}}(X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{0})
denotes the space of loops through the vertices
X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n}
and the equivalence follows from the previous remark and the fact that
\Pi_{1}
commutes with colimits. The geometric realization of this diagram of spaces is known as
unstable topological Hochschild homology
and has been computed to be the free loop space
(N_{2}\mathscr{G})^{S^{1}}

[
HS19
, Corollary 4, Page 858]
. Since
N_{2}
is fully faithful and
N_{2}B\mathbb{Z}=S^{1}
, it follows that

\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{G})\simeq(N_{2}\mathscr{G})^{S^{1}}=(N_{2}\mathscr{G})^{N_{2}B\mathbb{Z}}\simeq\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{B}\mathbb{Z},\mathscr{G}),

where the last equivalence follows from the fact that
N_{2}
is fully faithful.

Returning to the general case, we can use the explicit colimit presentation of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
(
Equation

3.3
) to better describe the category
\mathrm{Fun}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B}),\mathscr{D}\big)
of traces out of a bicategory
\mathscr{B}
into a category
\mathscr{D}
. Let
\big(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\big)^{\rhd}
be the join of
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
with the terminal category. Intuitively
\big(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\big)^{\rhd}
is the category
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
equipped with a new terminal object. Note there are evident inclusion functors
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to\big(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\big)^{\rhd}
and
[0]\to\big(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\big)^{\rhd}
, sending the unique object
0
to the terminal object we denote by
f
.

Definition 3.12
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a bicategory and
\mathscr{D}
a category. Let
\mathscr{B}_{*}:\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{\mathscr{B}_{0}})\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
be the colimit-preserving functor extending the functor
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{\mathscr{B}_{0}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
encoding the bicategory structure of
\mathscr{B}
.

The category
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
of
\mathscr{B}
-cocones taking values in
\mathscr{D}
has as objects the pseudofunctors
(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op})^{\rhd}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
that fit into the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op})^{\rhd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\{f\}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}_{*}\circ\mathscr{O}_{bihh}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}

and pseudonatural transformations as morphisms.

Concretely we can depict an object in
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
as a diagram in
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
of the form

(3.13)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}

Since the objects in
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
are pseudofunctors, the commutativity in
Equation

3.13
and
Equation

3.14
hold up to appropriate choices of natural isomorphisms. Understanding the precise data of objects in
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
is an important aspect of the proof of
3.19
. As this description is quite technical, it has been relegated to
Appendix

A
.

Remark 3.15
.

For every bicategory
\mathscr{B}
, there is a
\mathscr{B}
-cocone taking values in
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
, given by the maps into the colimit. As we observe below, this
\mathscr{B}
-cocone is universal, in the sense that every other
\mathscr{B}
-cocone factors through it.

The identification below follows immediately from the colimit description of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
(
Equation

3.3
) and the universal property of colimits.

Proposition 3.16
.

The functor

\mathrm{Comp}:\mathrm{Fun}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B}),\mathscr{D}\big)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D}),

which composes a functor
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{D}
with the universal
\mathscr{B}
-cocone taking values in
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
, is an equivalence of categories, which is natural in
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{D}
.

In other words, traces out of
\mathscr{B}
into
\mathscr{D}
(
2.17
) are equivalent to
\mathscr{B}
-cocones taking values in
\mathscr{D}
.

3.2.
The equivalence between shadows and traces

We are finally ready to establish the main result of this section, that shadows (
Section

2.1
) are traces of bicategories (
Section

2.2
). To state the result precisely, we first introduce a notion of morphisms between shadows.

Definition 3.17
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a bicategory and
\mathscr{D}
a category. The
category of shadow functors from
\mathscr{B}
to
\mathscr{D}
, denoted

\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
, is specified as follows.

(1)

The objects are shadows, i.e., pairs
\big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle,\theta\big)
satisfying the conditions of
2.3
.

(2)

Given two shadows
\big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1},\theta_{1}\big)
,
\big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2},\theta_{2}\big)
, a morphism between them consists of a natural transformation

\alpha_{-}:\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1}\to\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2}

such that for any pair of 1-morphisms
F:X_{0}\to X_{1}
,
G:X_{1}\to X_{0}
in
\mathscr{B}
, the diagram

(3.18)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle FG\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle GF\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle FG\rangle\rangle_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle GF\rangle\rangle_{2},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{FG}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{GF}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{2}}

in
\mathscr{D}
commutes, and
\alpha
commutes with associators and unitors as well.

Theorem 3.19
.

For any bicategory
\mathscr{B}
and category
\mathscr{D}
, there is an equivalence of categories

\mathrm{Fun}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B}),\mathscr{D}\big)\xrightarrow{\ \simeq\ }\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})

that factors via the functor
\mathrm{Comp}
(
3.16
) through the category
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
(
3.12
) and that is natural in
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{D}
. Here
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
denotes the category of shadow functors from
\mathscr{B}
to
\mathscr{D}
(
3.17
).

Before we proceed to the proof, we mention some formal, but valuable, implications of this theorem.

Corollary 3.20
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a bicategory. There is a universal shadow functor
\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{u}
from
\mathscr{B}
to
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
such that for every other shadow functor
\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle
from
\mathscr{B}
to
\mathscr{D}
, there is a unique functor
F:\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{D}
and equality
F_{*}\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{u}=\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle
.

Proof.

Let
\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{u}
be the shadow that corresponds to the identity functor
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
under the equivalence in
3.19
. The desired equality now follows from the fact that the following square commutes by naturality
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B}),\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{B}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B}),\mathscr{D})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

,

for any functor
F:\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{D}
.
∎

Corollary 3.21
.

For any bicategory
\mathscr{B}
, the functor
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},-):\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
is corepresentable and hence preserves limits.

We now move on to the proof of
3.19
.

Proof of
3.19
.

The proof is quite long, so we break it down into six steps.

Step (I) Reduction to
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
and
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}
:

By
3.16
the functor

\mathrm{Comp}:\mathrm{Fun}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B}),\mathscr{D}\big)\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})

is a natural equivalence of categories. In order to prove the theorem, we define two functors natural in
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{D}
that we think of as performing “strictification” and “unstrictification” processes (
3.28
),

\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})

and

\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}:\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D}),

and then prove that they are mutually inverse.

Step (II) The functor
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
on objects:

For any cocone
C=(C_{0},C_{1},C_{2})
in
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{ocone}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
, the functor underlying
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}(C)
is

C_{0}:\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})\to\mathscr{D}.

The natural isomorphism witnessing the trace-like property of
C_{0}
is the composite of the natural isomorphisms witnessing the commutativity of the lefthand triangles in
Equation

3.13
,

\theta_{C}:C_{0}d_{0}\xrightarrow{\cong}C_{1}\xrightarrow{\cong}C_{0}d_{1}.

Since this assignment is clearly natural in
\mathscr{B}
and
\mathscr{D}
, what remains is to prove that
(C_{0},\theta_{C})
satisfies the axioms of a shadow, i.e., that the two diagrams of
2.3
commute. First we show that
Equation

2.4
commutes.

The fact that
Equation

3.13
is a cocone means that the image of the map

\displaystyle C_{0}:\mathrm{Fun}^{\Delta}\big(\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0}),\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})\big)\to\mathrm{Fun}\big(\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0}),\mathscr{D}\big)

must be contractible (here we are using
3.6
). Concretely, this means the image under postcomposition with
C_{0}
of any two functors
\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0})\to\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})
induced by the simplicial operators must be naturally isomorphic in a
unique
way (as a non-unique natural isomorphism would give us a non-trivial loop).

Equation

3.13
gives rise to the cube below, in which each face commutes up to natural isomorphism.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}

There are exactly six paths in this cube from the top left corner to the bottom right corner,

\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0})\to\mathscr{D},

corresponding to the six objects in
Equation

2.4
. The natural isomorphisms on the faces of the cube correspond to the morphisms between the objects in
Equation

2.4
. As explained above, the diagram of natural isomorphisms must commute since
Equation

3.13
is a cocone.

It remains to prove that
Equation

2.5
commutes. By symmetry it suffices to verify commutativity of the left-hand triangle, which we do by an argument similar to that above. Since
Equation

3.13
is a cocone, the image of

C_{0}:\mathrm{Fun}^{\Delta}\big(\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0}),\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})\big)\to\mathrm{Fun}\big(\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0}),\mathscr{D}\big)

must be contractible, which means that any two functors from
\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})
to
\mathscr{D}
induced by the simplicial diagram in the domain functor category must be natural isomorphic in a unique manner.

Since the morphisms in the left-hand triangle in
Equation

2.5
are exactly the natural isomorphisms in the diagram below, we can conclude by the remark above that the triangle commutes as desired.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces id}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

Step (III) The functor
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
on morphisms:

We now explain how to define
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
on morphisms and show that it is indeed a functor.
Let

(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}):(C_{0},C_{1},C_{2})\Rightarrow(C_{0}^{\prime},C_{1}^{\prime},C_{2}^{\prime})

be a morphism of cocones. We just proved that the functors
C_{0}
and
C_{0}^{\prime}
can be equipped with natural transformations
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
with respect to which they are shadows. By definition

\alpha_{0}:C_{0}\to C_{0}^{\prime}

is a natural transformation. We need to check that
\alpha_{0}
satisfies the conditions formulated in
3.17
with respect to
\theta
and
\theta^{\prime}
, allowing us to set
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}(\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2})=\alpha_{0}
. Preservation of composition and of identities by

\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
is then obvious.

The compatibility of
\alpha_{0}
and
\alpha_{1}
with the natural isomorphisms in the cocones
C
and
C^{\prime}
implies that the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}d_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}d_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}^{\prime}d_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{1}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}^{\prime}d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

commutes and hence that
Equation

3.18
commutes. It remains to show that
\alpha_{0}
commutes with associators and unitors, which follows from the diagrams below, by standard whiskering arguments in bicategories, again using, as in the previous step, that
(C_{0},C_{1},C_{2})
and
(C^{\prime}_{0},C^{\prime}_{1},C^{\prime}_{2})
are cocones.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces a}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces l}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{0}}

Thus
\alpha_{0}
is indeed a morphism of shadows.

Step (IV) The functor
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}
on objects:

Given any shadow functor
\big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle,\theta\big)
from
\mathscr{B}
to
\mathscr{D}
, we construct a
\mathscr{B}
-cocone taking values in
\mathscr{D}
, using
Lemma

A.18
in the case where the target
2
-category is
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
, and the functor
F
is
\mathscr{O}_{bihh}
. We need thus to choose an appropriate category, functor, and natural isomorphism that satisfy the conditions stated in
Lemma

A.18
.

The extra condition on the extension in the definition of
\mathscr{B}
-cocones (
3.12
) implies that the category chosen as the image of the terminal object must be
\mathscr{D}
. The functor we choose as the image of
t_{0}:0\to f
is
\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle:\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})\to\mathscr{D}
, while the natural isomorphism chosen should clearly be that given as part of the shadow structure,
\theta:\langle\langle FG\rangle\rangle\cong\langle\langle GF\rangle\rangle
.

We need to prove that the two equalities of 2-cells stated in
Lemma

A.18
hold for the choices that we have made.

The first condition in
Lemma

A.18
translates to the following diagram

(3.22)

which commutes by
Equation

2.5
.

The second condition that the natural isomorphisms in
Lemma

A.18
must satisfy translates to the commutativity of the following diagram.

(3.23)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{2}(-)\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{1}(-)\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}d_{1}(-)\rangle\rangle}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}d_{2}(-)\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}

Since the commutativity of this diagram is exactly
Equation

2.4
in the definition of a shadow, we can conclude.

Step (V) The functor
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}
on morphisms:

We now extend
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}
to a functor. Let

\alpha:\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1}\to\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2}

be a morphism in
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}(\mathscr{B},\mathscr{D})
, i.e., a natural transformation such that the diagrams in
3.17
commute. Our goal is it to construct a natural transformation from
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1})\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2})
, which informally should have following shape.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{0})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},X_{1},X_{2}}\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{1},X_{2},X_{0}).}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{0}d_{0}}

More precisely, a natural transformation
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1})\to\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2})
is a pseudofunctor

\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\alpha:(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op})^{\rhd}\to\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})

that fits into the following diagram.

(3.24)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op})^{\rhd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{2}}

Here, the arrow labeled
\mathrm{id}
corresponds the identity natural transformation on the functor
\mathscr{B}_{*}\circ\mathscr{O}_{bihh}
(see
3.12
), which is the common value of the restrictions of
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1}
and
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2}
to
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
.
Our goal is thus to construct such a functor
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\alpha
.

To lift the functor
\mathrm{id}:({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})
, we again use
Lemma

A.18
. We proceed now to make the necessary choices and verify the necessary conditions. For the remainder of the proof, it is helpful to keep in mind that
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})
is a bicategory with functors
[1]\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
as objects and squares that commute up to natural isomorphisms as morphisms.

Considering the hypotheses of
Lemma

A.18
, this time with
\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})
as target bicategory, we choose the image of the final object to be the identity functor on
\mathscr{D}
and the image of the 1-cell
t_{0}:0\to f
to be
\alpha
itself, seen as filling in a square where the composites along the sides are
\mathrm{id}_{\mathscr{D}}\circ\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1}
and
\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2}\circ\mathrm{id}_{\coprod\mathscr{B}(X_{0},X_{0})}
. Given these choices, we must now find an appropriate natural isomorphism between
\alpha d_{0}
and
\alpha d_{1}
, for which we select the diagram

(3.25)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{2}}

which commutes by the conditions of a morphism of shadows (
3.17
).

We now need to verify the two conditions imposed on the natural isomorphisms from
Lemma

A.18
. The first one requires that the following diagram commute

(3.26)

whereas the second condition requires the diagram below to commute.

(3.27)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{2}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{2}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{1}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}d_{1}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}d_{2}(-)\rangle\rangle_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{0}d_{1}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}d_{1}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle d_{1}d_{2}(-)\rangle\rangle_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{0}d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{1}d_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{0}d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{0}d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{1}d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha d_{1}d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\langle\langle a\rangle\rangle_{2}}

To establish the commutativity of these two diagrams, we use that the outer squares and triangles in the diagram commute (by
3.17
), whence the inside diagram commutes as well, since categories are
2
-coskeletal.

Applying
Lemma

A.18
, we to deduce that we have indeed defined a functor
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\alpha:(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op})^{\rhd}\to\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at})
, which fits by construction into
Equation

3.24
, since
\pi_{1}\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}(\alpha)=\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{1})
and
\pi_{2}\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}(\alpha)=\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle_{2})
.

Step (VI):
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
and
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}
are mutually inverse:

We now prove that
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
and
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}
are inverse to each other.
If
\big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle,\theta\big)
is a shadow, then

\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\Big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle,\theta\Big)=\Big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle,\langle\langle d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle,\langle\langle d_{0}d_{0}(-)\rangle\rangle\Big)

and so

\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle,\theta\big)=\big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle,\theta\big),

i.e.,
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}
is the identity functor.

On the other hand, let
C=(C_{0},C_{1},C_{2})
be an arbitrary cocone. By
A.17
this cocone (which is by definition a pseudofunctor) is naturally equivalent to a strict cocone, which, by
Lemma

A.18
is of the form
(C_{0},C_{0}d_{0},C_{0}d_{0}d_{0})
. By functoriality
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
and
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}
preserve natural isomorphisms, so it suffices to prove that
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}
takes this particular cocone to itself.

This follows by direct computation. Indeed
\mathscr{S}\mathrm{t}(C_{0},C_{0}d_{0},C_{0}d_{0}d_{0})
is a shadow with shadow functor
C_{0}
and
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}
, by its very definition, takes this shadow to the cocone
(C_{0},C_{0}d_{0},C_{0}d_{0}d_{0})
.
∎

Remark 3.28
.

The proof above can be thought of as a “strictification argument”. We strictify a general diagram such that the total amount of data reduces to that of a shadow. The proof shows that no data is lost in this strictification process. A shadow can thus be characterized as the minimal amount of data required to describe a cocone.

Remark 3.29
.

Applying
3.19
to the shadow on the bicategory
\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od})
of
2.6
gives rise to a trace (
2.17
) out of
\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od})
into
\mathrm{Ho}(\mathrm{Sp})
,

\mathrm{THH}:\mathrm{biHH}\big(\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od})\big)\to\mathrm{Ho}(\mathrm{Sp}).

We generalize this result to
\infty
-categorical bimodules in
6.11
.

4.
Morita Invariance of Shadows via Hochschild Homology

An important property satisfied by
\mathrm{THH}
of ring spectra is
Morita invariance
: if two ring spectra
R
and
R^{\prime}
are Morita equivalent (i.e., their model categories of modules are Quillen equivalent), then
\mathrm{THH}(R)\simeq\mathrm{THH}(R^{\prime})

[
BM12
]
. In line with the idea that a shadow is an axiomatic, bicategorical generalization of
\mathrm{THH}
, Campbell and Ponto proved that shadows satisfied a natural generalization of Morita invariance
[
CP19
, Proposition 4.6]
. In this section we recover Morita invariance of shadows using the machinery we developed in the previous sections, which enables us to define a functorial lift of the useful Euler characteristic construction of Campbell and Ponto, whose work we begin by reviewing.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a bicategory equipped with a shadow functor
\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle
taking values in a category
\mathscr{D}
. Given an adjunction in
\mathscr{B}
, i.e., a diagram

(4.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces GF}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces FG}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}

of 0-, 1-, and 2-cells in
\mathscr{B}
satisfying the triangle identities, the
Euler characteristic

\chi(F)
of the
1
-cell
F
is defined to be the composite morphism

\langle\langle U_{C}\rangle\rangle\xrightarrow{\langle\langle u\rangle\rangle}\langle\langle GF\rangle\rangle\cong\langle\langle FG\rangle\rangle\xrightarrow{\langle\langle c\rangle\rangle}\langle\langle U_{D}\rangle\rangle

in
\mathscr{D}
.

Morita invariance of shadows is formulated as follows in terms of the Euler characteristic.

Proposition 4.2

(
[
CP19
, Proposition 4.6]
)
.

If the adjunction in
4.1
is actually an equivalence, i.e., if the 2-cells
c
and
u
are invertible, then
\chi(F)
is an isomorphism in
\mathscr{D}
with inverse given by
\chi(G)
.

This result can be generalized to arbitrary endomorphisms. For a given endomorphism
Q:C\to C
, there is a chain of morphisms

\langle\langle Q\rangle\rangle\cong\langle\langle Q\mathrm{U}_{C}\rangle\rangle\xrightarrow{\ \langle\langle u\mathrm{id}_{Q}\rangle\rangle\ }\langle\langle GFQ\rangle\rangle\xrightarrow{\langle\langle\mathrm{id}_{GFQ}u\rangle\rangle}\langle\langle GFQGF\rangle\rangle\cong\langle\langle FQGFG\rangle\rangle\xrightarrow{\langle\langle c\rangle\rangle}\langle\langle FQG\rangle\rangle,

which is known as the
trace of
Q
(which is different from the trace defined in
2.17
) and denoted
\mathrm{tr}(u_{Q})
. Now, we have the following result due to Campbell and Ponto.

Proposition 4.3

(
[
CP19
, Proposition 4.8]
)
.

If the adjunction in
4.1
is actually an equivalence, i.e., if the 2-cells
c
and
u
are invertible, then
\mathrm{tr}(u_{Q})
is an isomorphism in
\mathscr{D}
with inverse given by
\mathrm{tr}(c_{Q})
.

We provide an alternative proof of these results below, exploiting the equivalence between shadows and functors out of Hochschild homology, which leads to a formal, functorial construction of
\chi
and
\mathrm{tr}
. Since by
3.19
, a shadow out of
\mathscr{B}
is equivalent to a functor out of the category
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
, we can focus the proof of Morita invariance on the representing object.

Definition 4.4
.

Let
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
be the free strict bicategory generated by the two objects, two
1
-morphisms and two
2
-morphisms given in
4.1
and relation given by the triangle equalities.

For more details regarding the
2
-category
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
see the original description in
[
SS86
]
.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}
be the strict bicategory with the same objects,
1
-cells and
2
-cells as
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
, but where all
2
-morphisms are invertible.

As in
4.1
, a bifunctor out of
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}
can be depicted as a diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g\circ f}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f\circ g}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces u}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Definition 4.6
.

For any bicategory
\mathscr{B}
, let

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(\mathscr{B})=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj},\mathscr{B})

and

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}(\mathscr{B})=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q},\mathscr{B}),

the categories of
adjunctions in
\mathscr{B}
and of
adjoint equivalences in
\mathscr{B}
, respectively.

There is an evident localization functor
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}
that induces a restriction functor

(4.7)

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(\mathscr{B}).

To complete our set-up, we introduce the following notation.

Notation 4.8
.

For any category
\mathscr{D}
, let

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rr}(\mathscr{D})=\mathrm{Fun}([1],\mathscr{D}),

and let
\mathscr{I}\mathrm{so}(\mathscr{D})
denote the full subcategory of
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rr}(\mathscr{D})
consisting of isomorphisms.

Theorem 4.9
.

For any bicategory
\mathscr{B}
, there is a functor

\widehat{\chi}:\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rr}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\big)

that takes an adjunction
\sigma=(C,D,F,G,u,c)
to the morphism

\widehat{\chi}(\sigma):\mathrm{U}_{C}\xrightarrow{u}GF\xrightarrow{\cong}FG\xrightarrow{c}\mathrm{U}_{D}

in
\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{B})
.

Note that it is essential here that the codomain of this functor is the category of morphisms in
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
, which ensures the existence of the isomorphism
GF\xrightarrow{\cong}FG
.

Proof.

The functoriality of
\mathrm{biHH}
for enrichments in
\mathscr{V}=(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at},\times)
(
3.4
) implies that there is a functor

\mathrm{biHH}:\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathrm{Fun}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}),\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\big)

The explicit colimit description of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})
as a colimit in
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
(
3.4
) implies that
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})
in particular has the objects the two endomorphisms
\mathrm{U}_{0},\mathrm{U}_{1}
, has the
2

1
-morphisms
u:\mathrm{U}_{0}\to gf
and
c:fg\to\mathrm{U}_{1}
and an isomorphism
fg\cong gf
.

Hence, there is a morphism
\alpha:[1]\to\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})
, that picks out the morphism
\mathrm{U}_{0}\to gf\cong fg\to\mathrm{U}_{1}
. The existence of the desired functor now follows from precomposing with
\alpha
.
∎

Remark 4.10
.

Notice
4.9
will generalize to an arbitrary
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category, as soon as the functoriality of
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}
is established in this case.

The corollary below is an immediate consequence of the fact that every functor preserves isomorphisms.

Corollary 4.11
.

Precomposing the functor
\widehat{\chi}:\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rr}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\big)
with the restriction functor
