\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(\mathscr{B})
given in
4.7
gives rise to a functor

\widehat{\chi}:\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{I}\mathrm{so}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\big).

From
4.11
we can recover the Morita invariance of shadows, proven originally in
[
CP19
, Proposition 4.6]
.

Proposition 4.12
.

Shadows satisfy Morita invariance.

Proof.

Let
\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle
be a shadow functor on
\mathscr{B}
. Consider its unstrictification
(
3.19
), which is a functor
\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle\big):\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{D}
. Precomposing this with the functor
\widehat{\chi}
of
4.9
gives rise to a functor

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rr}\Big(\mathscr{U}\mathrm{n}\big(\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle\big)\Big)\circ\widehat{\chi}:\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rr}(\mathscr{D})

that takes an adjunction in
\mathscr{B}
to the morphism

\langle\langle U_{C}\rangle\rangle\to\langle\langle GF\rangle\rangle\cong\langle\langle FG\rangle\rangle\to\langle\langle U_{D}\rangle\rangle

in
\mathscr{D}
.
The desired conclusion now follows from
4.11
.
∎

We now want to move on to the more general case and study traces of endomorphisms
\mathrm{tr}(u_{Q})
. This requires us to have a better understanding of the
2
-category with one adjunction and one endomorphism.

Definition 4.13
.

Let
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}
be the free category with the same objects,
1
-morphisms and
2
-morphisms of
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
along with one additional free endomorphism
[0]\to[0]
. Define
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}
similarly along with its localization map
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}
.

Remark 4.14
.

If we denote the free endomorphism by
q:0\to 0
an arbitrary endomorphism
0\to 0
in
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}
is a word on the two letter
q
and
gf
, whereas a free endomorphism on
1
is a word on
fg
and
fqg
.

Definition 4.15
.

For a bicategory
\mathscr{B}
, let
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd},\mathscr{B})
and
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})=\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd},\mathscr{B})
and notice we again have an inclusion functor
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})
.

Notice, as we have a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{[0]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces B\mathbb{N}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}}

and a similar one for
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}
and so

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})\simeq\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(\mathscr{B})\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B}),

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})\simeq\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}(\mathscr{B})\times_{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B}).

Using this description we can now define the desired functor.

Theorem 4.16
.

For any bicategory
\mathscr{B}
, there is a functor

\widehat{\mathrm{tr}}:\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rr}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\big)

that takes an adjunction
\sigma=(C,D,F,G,u,c)
and endomorphism
Q:C\to C
to the morphism

\widehat{\mathrm{tr}}(\sigma,Q):Q\xrightarrow{uQu}GFQFG\xrightarrow{\cong}FQGFG\xrightarrow{FQGc}FQG

in
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
.

Proof.

Again functoriality of
\mathrm{biHH}
(
3.4
) implies that there is a functor

\displaystyle\mathrm{biHH}:\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})

\displaystyle\to\mathrm{Fun}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}),\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{B})\big).

Now, again the explicit description of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
(
3.4
) and the description of endomorphisms in
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}
(
4.14
) implies that
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
in particular has
1
-morphisms
uqu:q\to gfqfg
and
fqgc:fqgfg\to fqg
, and there exists an isomorphism
gfqgf\cong fqgfg
. Let
\beta:[1]\to\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
be the functor that picks out the composition of
uqu
and
fqgc
(with the isomorphism in between). Then we obtain the desired functor by precomposing with
\beta
.
∎

We again have the following result analogous to
4.11
.

Corollary 4.17
.

Precomposing the functor
\widehat{\mathrm{tr}}:\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{rr}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\big)
with the restriction functor
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})
given in
4.15
gives rise to a functor

\widehat{\chi}:\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{q}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{I}\mathrm{so}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})\big).

Finally, we can now recover
[
CP19
, Proposition 4.8]
using the same argument we used in
4.12
relying on
3.19
.

Proposition 4.18
.

Let
\langle\langle-\rangle\rangle
be a shadow on a bicategory
\mathscr{B}
valued in a
\mathscr{D}
. Then for a given Morita equivalence
\sigma=(C,D,F,G,u,c)
and endomorphism
Q:C\to C
, the map
\mathrm{tr}(u_{Q}):\langle\langle Q\rangle\rangle\to\langle\langle FQG\rangle\rangle
is an isomorphism in
\mathscr{D}
with inverse
\mathrm{tr}(c_{Q})
.

5.
Hochschild Homology of
2
-Categories and Homotopy Coherence

In the previous section we exploited the structure two bicategories, namely
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
and
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}
, to deduce Morita invariance or invariance of the Euler characteristic and trace, presenting alternative proofs to the ones in
[
CP19
]
. However, this alternative approach naturally results in several interesting questions:

(1)

The construction of the Euler characteristic and trace involves a choice of isomorphism. Is the choice of isomorphism canonical or depend on choices?

(2)

We construct the Euler characteristic and trace by restricting to a certain morphism in the categories
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})
and
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
. Would it be possible to deduce other invariance properties of shadows by restricting to other morphisms in those categories?

Answering these type of questions necessitates having a clear understanding of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})
and
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
far beyond the ones used in
Section

4
. Hence, in this section we completely compute those two categories (
5.23
/
5.26
), which we then use to address the first question (
5.25
/
5.28
), leaving the second one for future work. The key towards all these computations is a very explicit description of Hochschild homology of strict bicategories, which is the content of
5.10
and requires a technical analysis.

Recall that for any small category
\mathscr{C}
and cocomplete category
\mathscr{D}
, there is a
tensor (or coend) functor

-\otimes-:\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})\times\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{C}^{op},\mathscr{D})\to\mathscr{D}

that takes a a pair
(F,G)
to the coequalizer of
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{f:c\to c^{\prime}}F(c)\times G(c^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{c}F(c)\times G(c)}

.

Remark 5.1
.

The tensor functor defined above satisfies the following properties.

•

[
Rie14
, Example 4.1.3]
: If
\mathscr{D}
has a terminal object and
F:\mathscr{C}\to\mathscr{D}
is the terminal functor, then
F\otimes G=\mathrm{colim}_{\mathscr{C}^{op}}G
.

•

[
Rie14
, Example 4.1.5]
: If
F=\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}}(c,-)
, then
F\otimes G\cong G(c)
.

•

The tensor functor preserves colimits.

For more details regarding the tensor functor, see
[
Rie14
, Section 4.1]
.

Below we provide a more explicit description of pseudo-colimits in terms of the tensor functor, for which the following technical result proves useful.

Lemma 5.2
.

The tensor functor

-\otimes-:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}^{{\mathbbe{\Delta}}}\times\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}^{{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}

is left Quillen, with respect to the canonical model structure on the codomain and the Reedy model structure on each factor of the domain.

Remark 5.3
.

The canonical model structure on
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
is Cartesian, and

•

the cofibrations are functors that are injective on objects, and

•

weak equivalences are categorical equivalences.

See also
[
Rie14
, Example 11.3.9]
for more details.

Proof.

Since the canonical model structure on
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
is Cartesian, we can conclude by an argument analogous to that in
[
Hir03
, 18.4.11]
. See also the discussion in
[
Rie14
, Section 14.3]
.
∎

To use this lemma to describe pseudo-colimits, we need the following definition.

Definition 5.4
.

Let
I[n]
denote the category with
n
objects and a unique morphism between any two objects (which in particular implies that all morphisms are isomorphisms). The collection of all
I[n]
underlies a cosimplicial category

I[\bullet]:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}.

Proposition 5.5
.

If
F:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
is a strict functor, then the pseudo-colimit of
F
is equivalent to the tensor product
I[\bullet]\otimes F
.

Proof.

By
[
Gam08
]
the pseudo-colimit of
F
coincides with the homotopy colimit of
F
in the canonical model structure on
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
. By
5.1
the colimit of
F
is given by
*\otimes F
and so, by
Lemma

5.2
, the homotopy colimit is given by left deriving this functor.

Observe that
F:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
is already Reedy cofibrant. Indeed, the latching object
L_{n}F
is a full subcategory of
F_{n}
and so by construction
L_{n}F\to F_{n}
is a cofibration (
5.3
). We need thus only to find a Reedy cofibrant replacement of the terminal diagram.

We claim that
I[\bullet]:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
(
5.4
) is such a cofibrant replacement. The map to
I[\bullet]\to*
is obviously a level-wise weak equivalence (
5.3
), so it suffices to show that
I[\bullet]
is Reedy cofibrant. This follows from direct computation, as the latching object
L_{n}I
has
n+1
objects, and the map
L_{n}I\to I[n]
is the identity on objects and hence a cofibration (
5.3
).
∎

We next provide a more explicit description of
I[\bullet]\otimes F
. Recall there is an adjunction

(5.6)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{s}\mathscr{S}\text{et}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bot}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N}

where the right adjoint is the nerve functor, and the left adjoint is known as the
fundamental category
.

Remark 5.7
.

The fundamental category can be described explicitly as follows.

•

\mathrm{Obj}_{\tau_{1}X}=X_{0}
.

•

The set
X_{1}
generates the morphisms of
\tau_{1}X
.

•

The morphisms of
\tau_{1}X
satisfy the relations
d_{1}\sigma\sim d_{0}\sigma\circ d_{2}\sigma
for all
\sigma\in X_{2}
and
s_{0}x\sim\mathrm{id}_{x}
for all
x\in X_{0}
.

See
[
Rez17
, Section 11]
for more details.

The following simple property of the fundamental category proves useful to us below.

Lemma 5.8
.

For all functors
F:J\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
,

\mathrm{colim}_{J}F\cong\tau_{1}\mathrm{colim}_{J}N(F).

In particular, for every strict functor
F:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
,

I[\bullet]\otimes F\cong\tau_{1}(NI[\bullet]\otimes NF).

Proof.

For any category
\mathscr{D}
, there is a chain of isomorphisms

\displaystyle\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}}\big(\tau_{1}\mathrm{colim}_{J}N(F),\mathscr{D}\big)

\displaystyle\cong\mathrm{Hom}_{\text{s}\mathscr{S}\text{et}}\big(\mathrm{colim}_{J}N(F),N\mathscr{D}\big)

\displaystyle\text{adjunction}(\tau_{1},N)

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{J}\mathrm{Hom}_{\text{s}\mathscr{S}\text{et}}\big(N(F)(j),N\mathscr{D}\big)

definition of colimit

\displaystyle\cong\lim_{J}\mathrm{Hom}_{\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}}\big(F(j),\mathscr{D}\big)

N
fully faithful,

which implies that
\tau_{1}\mathrm{colim}_{J}N(F)
satisfies the universal property of a colimit, and we can conclude.
∎

By definition
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
is a pseudo-colimit and we now use our newly gained understanding of pseudo-colimits of categories to give a more precise characterization of this construction via generators and relations.

For the next proof, recall that if
\mathscr{B}
is a
2
-category, then for every object
X
, there is a unit object
\mathrm{U}_{X}
in
\mathscr{B}(X,X)
. Additionally, for every
1
-morphism
F
in
\mathscr{B}(X,Y)
, there is an identity
2
-morphism
\mathrm{id}_{F}
. Moreover, the composition of two
1
-morphisms or
2
-morphisms in
\mathscr{B}(X,Y)
and
\mathscr{B}(Y,Z)
is denoted via
\ast
, to distinguish it from the composition internal to the categories
\mathscr{B}(X,Y)
.

Remark 5.9
.

For the next theorem, we need a detailed understanding of the bisimplicial set
(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{\bullet\bullet}
and so we present here an explicit diagram for the benefit of the reader.

For a category
\mathscr{C}
, let
\mathrm{Comp}_{\mathscr{C}}
denote the set of composable morphisms
(f,g)
such that
\mathrm{Dom}_{g}=\mathrm{Cod}_{f}
. Now, using
3.1
, we can depict the bisimplicial set
(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{\bullet\bullet}
as follows with certain morphisms described explicitly.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(X,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X,Y\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(X,Y)\times\mathscr{B}(Y,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X,Y,Z\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(X,Y)\times\mathscr{B}(Y,Z)\times\mathscr{B}(Z,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}(X,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X,Y\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}(X,Y)\times\mathscr{B}(Y,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X,Y,Z\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}(X,Y)\times\mathscr{B}(Y,Z)\times\mathscr{B}(Z,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Comp}_{\mathscr{B}(X,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X,Y\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Comp}_{\mathscr{B}(X,Y)\times\mathscr{B}(Y,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{X,Y,Z\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Comp}_{\mathscr{B}(X,Y)\times\mathscr{B}(Y,Z)\times\mathscr{B}(Z,X)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \vdots\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \vdots\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \vdots\ }
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,\mathrm{U})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{id}_{(F,G)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces G\ast F}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\ast G}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F,G\ast H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(F\ast G,H)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(H\ast F,G)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Dom}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{Cod}_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha,\mathrm{id}_{\mathrm{U}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{Dom}_{\alpha},\mathrm{Dom}_{\beta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathrm{Cod}_{\alpha},\mathrm{Cod}_{\beta})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta\ast\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha\ast\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha,\beta\ast\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha\ast\beta,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma\ast\alpha,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha_{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{2}\circ\beta_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta_{1}\ast\alpha_{1},\beta_{2}\ast\alpha_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{1}\ast\beta_{1},\alpha_{2}\ast\beta_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{2}\circ\alpha_{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{2}\circ\beta_{1},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{2}\circ\gamma_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\alpha_{2},\beta_{2},\gamma_{2})}

Theorem 5.10
.

If
\mathscr{B}
is any
2
-category, then
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
admits the following presentation.

•

\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})}=\coprod_{X\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(X,X)}

•

Generating morphisms

(1)

Symbols
\alpha:\mathrm{Dom}_{\alpha}\to\mathrm{Cod}_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha\in\coprod_{X\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}}\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}(X,X)}
.

(2)

Symbols
(F,G):F*G\to G*F
for all
F\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(X,Y)}
,
G\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(Y,X)}
and all
X,Y\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}}
.

•

Relations

(1)

(F,\mathrm{U}_{X}):F\to F
is the identity morphism of the object
F\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(X,X)}
.

(2)

The composite of a pair of symbols
\alpha,\beta\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}(X,X)}
such that
\mathrm{Dom}_{\beta}=\mathrm{Cod}_{\alpha}
is equal to their composite
\beta\circ\alpha
in the category
\mathscr{B}(X,X)
.

(3)

All symbols
(F,G)
are isomorphisms.

(4)

For all symbols
\alpha:F\to G
and
\beta:H\to L
in
\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}(X,X)}
,

(G,L)\circ(\alpha\ast\beta)=(\beta\ast\alpha)\circ(F,H).

(5)

For any three
1
-morphisms
F\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(X,Y)}
,
G\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(Y,Z)}
,
H\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(Z,X)}
,

(F,G\ast H)=(H\ast F,G)\circ(F\ast G,H).

Proof.

According to
2.10
,
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
is the colimit of the simplicial diagram
\mathscr{B}_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{\bullet}
. Because
\mathscr{B}
is a
2
-category,
\mathscr{B}_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{thh})_{\bullet}
is a strict diagram rather than just a pseudo-diagram, whence by
5.5
,
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
is equivalent to the tensor
I[\bullet]\otimes\mathscr{B}_{*}(\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{\bullet}
, which is isomorphic to the fundamental category of
NI[\bullet]\otimes(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{\bullet}
by
Lemma

5.8
.

In order to evaluate the fundamental category we need to better understand levels
0,1,
and
2
of the simplicial set
NI[\bullet]\otimes(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{\bullet}
. For a fixed
k\geq 0
, the evaluation map
(-)_{k}:\text{s}\mathscr{S}\text{et}\to\mathscr{S}\text{et}
preserves colimits, so there is a bijection of sets

(N(I[\bullet])\otimes N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{k}\cong NI[\bullet]_{k}\otimes(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{k}.

The following general observation is useful to us below. Let
\Delta[\bullet]:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}\to\text{s}\mathscr{S}\text{et}
be the Yoneda embedding. For every bisimplicial set
X
,

(5.11)

\Delta[\bullet]_{n}\otimes X_{k}\cong\Delta[n]\otimes X_{k}\cong X_{nk},

where the last step follows from
5.1
, as
\Delta[n]
is a representable functor.
In particular,

(\Delta[\bullet]\otimes X)_{k}\cong X_{kk},

i.e.,
\Delta[\bullet]\otimes X
is the diagonal of the bisimplicial set
X
.

If
k=0
, then
I[\bullet]_{0}=\Delta[\bullet]_{0}
, the representable functor, and so by the argument above

\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{B})}=(NI[\bullet]\otimes N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{0}=(N\Delta[\bullet]\otimes N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{0}=(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{00}=\coprod_{X}\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{B}(X,X)},

as desired.

If
k=1
, then
NI[\bullet]_{1}=\mathrm{Mor}_{I[\bullet]}=\Delta[\bullet]_{1}\coprod_{\Delta[\bullet]_{0}}\Delta[\bullet]_{1}
, where the pushout is that of the degeneracy map
s_{0}:\Delta[\bullet]_{0}\to\Delta[\bullet]_{1}
with itself, since any two objects of
I[n]
are connected by a unique isomorphism. Hence,

\displaystyle(NI[\bullet]\otimes N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{1}

\displaystyle\cong N(I[\bullet])_{1}\otimes(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{1}

\displaystyle\cong(\Delta[\bullet]_{1}\coprod_{\Delta[\bullet]_{0}}\Delta[\bullet]_{1})\otimes(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{1}

\displaystyle\cong(\Delta[\bullet]_{1}\otimes N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{1}\coprod_{(\Delta[\bullet]_{0}\otimes N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{1}}(\Delta[\bullet]_{1}\otimes N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{1}

\displaystyle\cong(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{11}\coprod_{(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{01}}(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{11},

where the last step follows from
5.11
.

Recall from
5.9
that
(N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{11}=\coprod_{X,Y}\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}(X,Y)\times\mathscr{B}(Y,X)}
, i.e., its elements are pairs
(\alpha,\beta)
with
\alpha:F\to G
and
\beta:H\to L
, with face maps made explicit in
5.9
. It follows that the set
(NI[\bullet]\otimes N\mathscr{B}_{*}\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{1}
consists of pairs
(\alpha,\beta):\mathrm{Dom}_{\alpha}\ast\mathrm{Dom}_{\beta}\to\mathrm{Cod}_{\beta}\ast\mathrm{Cod}_{\alpha}
and
(\alpha,\beta^{-1}):\mathrm{Dom}_{\beta}\ast\mathrm{Dom}_{\alpha}\to\mathrm{Cod}_{\alpha}\ast\mathrm{Cod}_{\beta}
, such that for every
\alpha\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}}(X,X)
we have
(\alpha,\mathrm{U}_{X})=(\alpha,\mathrm{U}_{X}^{-1}):\mathrm{Dom}_{\alpha}\to\mathrm{Cod}_{\alpha}
.

We now use the information in level
2
to describe the various relations between the morphisms. Notice that

NI[\bullet]_{2}\cong NI[\bullet]_{1}\times_{NI[\bullet]_{0}}NI[\bullet]_{1}.

Hence, the elements in the set
(NI[\bullet]\otimes N\mathscr{O}_{bihh})_{2}
are of the form

•

((\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}),(\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))
,

•

((\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}),(\beta_{0}^{-1},\beta_{1}^{\pm 1}),(\gamma_{0}^{\pm 1},\gamma_{1}^{\pm 1}))
,

•

((\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}),(\beta_{0}^{\pm 1},\beta_{1}^{\pm 1}),(\gamma_{0}^{\pm 1},\gamma_{1}^{\pm 1}))
,

•

((\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}),(\beta_{0}^{\pm 1},\beta_{1}^{\pm 1}),(\gamma_{0}^{\pm 1},\gamma_{1}^{\pm 1}))
,

where
\alpha_{0}:F_{0}\to F_{1},\alpha_{1}:F_{1}\to F_{2}\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}}(X,Y)
,
\beta_{0}:G_{0}\to G_{1},\beta_{1}:G_{1}\to G_{2}\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}}(Y,Z)
and
\gamma_{0}:H_{0}\to H_{1},\gamma_{1}:H_{1}\to H_{2}\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}}(Z,X)
.

We will first focus on relations induced by elements of the form
((\alpha_{0}\alpha_{1}),(\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))
. Using the explicit description given in
5.9
we have the following boundaries:

•

d_{0}((\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}),(\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))=(\gamma_{1}\ast\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})

•

d_{1}((\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}),(\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))=(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0},\beta_{1}\beta_{0}\ast\gamma_{1}\gamma_{0})

•

d_{2}((\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1}),(\beta_{0},\beta_{1}),(\gamma_{0},\gamma_{1}))=(\alpha_{0}\ast\beta_{0},\gamma_{0})

which means that we have the relation

(5.12)

(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0},\beta_{1}\beta_{0}\ast\gamma_{1}\gamma_{0})=(\gamma_{1}\ast\alpha_{1},\beta_{1})\circ(\alpha_{0}\ast\beta_{0},\gamma_{0}).

Fix
\alpha:F\to G\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}}(X,Y)
and
\beta:H\to L\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}}(Y,X)
.
Letting
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0})=(\alpha,\beta,\mathrm{U}_{X})
and
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1})=(\mathrm{id}_{G},\mathrm{id}_{L},\mathrm{U}_{X})
we get
(\alpha,\beta)=(\mathrm{id}_{G},\mathrm{id}_{L})\circ(\alpha\ast\beta,\mathrm{U}_{X})
. On the other side, letting
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0})=(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{H},\mathrm{U}_{X})
and
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1})=(\alpha,\beta,\mathrm{U}_{X})
we get
(\alpha,\beta)=(\beta\ast\alpha,\mathrm{U}_{X})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{H})
. Hence we have

(5.13)

(\alpha,\beta)=(\mathrm{id}_{G},\mathrm{id}_{L})\circ(\alpha\ast\beta,\mathrm{U}_{X})=(\beta\ast\alpha,\mathrm{U}_{X})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{H})

Now, by symmetry, we can repeat the same arguments for
(\alpha,\beta^{-1})
to conclude that

(5.14)

(\alpha,\beta^{-1})=(\alpha\ast\beta,\mathrm{U}_{X})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{H}^{-1})=(\mathrm{id}_{G},\mathrm{id}_{L}^{-1})\circ(\beta\ast\alpha,\mathrm{U}_{X})

We will denote the morphisms of the form
(\alpha,\mathrm{U}_{X})
by the symbol
\alpha
and morphisms of the form
(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G})
by
(F,G)
and morphisms of the form
(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G}^{-1})
by
(F,G)^{-1}
.

In
5.13
and
5.14
we have already established that every arbitrary morphism in
\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{B})
is generated by these three classes of morphisms. In order to finish the proof we need to understand how these morphisms interact with each other and in particular confirm the relations from the statement.

(1)

The first relation follows from the definition of the fundamental category.

(2)

Let
\alpha_{0}:F\to G,\alpha_{1}:G\to H\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{B}}(X,X)
be two morphisms. The Equation
5.12
with
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0})=(\alpha_{0},\mathrm{U}_{X},\mathrm{U}_{X})
and
(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1})=(\alpha_{1},\mathrm{U}_{X},\mathrm{U}_{X})
implies that

(\alpha_{1}\alpha_{0},\mathrm{U}_{X})=(\alpha_{1},\mathrm{U}_{X})\circ(\alpha_{0},\mathrm{U}_{X}),

which proves the second equation.

(3)

Now for two objects
F:X\to Y
and
G:Y\to X
we have by definition of
I[2]
a
2
-cell that we denote
(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G},\mathrm{id}_{G}^{-1})
that witnesses the composition
(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G}^{-1})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G})=(\mathrm{id}_{FG},\mathrm{U}_{X})
. We can similarly deduce that
(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G}^{-1})=(\mathrm{id}_{GF},\mathrm{U}_{Y})
. This proves that
(F,G)
is in fact an isomorphism with inverse
(F,G)^{-1}

(4)

We already confirmed the fourth condition in
5.13
.

(5)

Finally, we want to understand when two morphisms of the form
(F,G)
commute. Plugging in
(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},\gamma_{0})=(\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\gamma_{1})=(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G},\mathrm{id}_{H})
into Equation
5.12
we get

(\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G}\ast\mathrm{id}_{H})=(\mathrm{id}_{H}\ast\mathrm{id}_{F},\mathrm{id}_{G})\circ(\mathrm{id}_{F}\ast\mathrm{id}_{G},\mathrm{id}_{H}),

which gives us the desired relation
(F,G\ast H)=(H\ast F,G)\circ(F\ast G,H)
.

As we have checked all possible relations between all generating morphisms we have a complete characterization of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})
and hence we are done.
∎

In certain cases we can simplify the result in
5.10
further. For a given strict monoidal category
\mathscr{C}
, let
B\mathscr{C}
be the category with on object
*
and
B\mathscr{C}(*,*)=\mathscr{C}
.

Corollary 5.15
.

Let
(\mathscr{C},\otimes,\mathrm{U})
be a strict monoidal category. Then
\mathrm{biHH}(B\mathscr{C})
permits the following presentation:

•

\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{B})}=\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}}

•

Generating Morphisms

–

Symbols
\alpha:\mathrm{Dom}_{\alpha}\to\mathrm{Cod}_{\alpha}
where
\alpha\in\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{C}}
.

–

Symbols
(X,Y):X\otimes Y\to Y\otimes X
where
X,Y\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}}

•

Subject to the Relations

–

(X,\mathrm{U}):X\to X
is the identity morphism of the object
X\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}}
.

–

For two symbols
\alpha,\beta
coming from
\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{C}}
such that
\mathrm{Dom}_{\beta}=\mathrm{Cod}_{\alpha}
, the composition is given by the composition
\beta\circ\alpha
given in
\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{C}}
.

–

The symbols
(X,Y)
are isomorphisms.

–

For symbols
\alpha:X\to Y
and
\beta:Z\to W
in
\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{C}}
, we have

(Y,W)\circ\alpha\otimes\beta=\beta\otimes\alpha\circ(X,Z)
.

–

For three objects
X,Y,Z\in\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{C}}
we have the equality

(X,Y\otimes Z)=(Z\otimes X,Y)\circ(X\otimes Y,Z)
.

Proof.

Follows directly from applying
5.10
to the
2
-category
B\mathscr{C}
with only one object.
∎

We now want to apply this result to some key examples, which is the goal of the remainder of this section.

Proposition 5.16
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{biHH}(B\mathbb{N})\simeq\{0\}\coprod_{n\in\{1,2,3,...\}}\{n\}\times B\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

We apply
5.15
to symmetric monoidal category
\mathbb{N}
with only identity morphisms. It has objects
\{0,1,2,...\}
and no non-trivial morphisms. Moreover, for every
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
there is an isomorphism
(n,m):n+m\to n+m
.

Now, the last relation in
5.15
implies that for all
m>1

(n,m)=(n,(m-1)+1)=(1+n,m)\circ(n+m,1).

By induction, this implies that
(n,m)=(n,1)^{m}
and so every object
n>0
has a unique automorphism
(n-1,1)
. This finishes the proof.
∎

Proposition 5.17
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{biHH}(B(\mathbb{N}*\mathbb{N}))\simeq\{(0,0)\}\coprod_{(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\backslash\{(0,0)\}}\{(n,m)\}\times B\mathbb{Z}.

Proof.

Again, we want to use
5.15
for the symmetric monoidal category
B(\mathbb{N}\ast\mathbb{N})
. According to the result the objects are isomorphic to the elements in
\mathbb{N}*\mathbb{N}
. However, for two elements
x,y\in\mathbb{N}*\mathbb{N}
we have
xy\cong yx
in the category
\mathrm{biHH}(B(\mathbb{N}\ast\mathbb{N}))
and so it suffices to take one object from each isomorphism class, which corresponds to the commutator classes and are precisely
\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}
.

Now for a given object
(n,m)
an automorphism is given by a tuple
((n_{1},m_{1}),(n_{2},m_{2}))
such that
n=n_{1}+n_{2}
and
m=m_{1}+m_{2}=m_{2}+m_{1}
. If
n=0
or
m=0
, then this reduces to
5.16
and it follows that there is a unique generating automorphism.

If
n,m\neq 0
, then the only elements that commute with
(n,m)
are
((n,m),(0,0))
and
((0,0),(n,m))
. The first is the identity, by
5.15
, and so we again have one free automorphism and so the desired result follows.
∎

Remark 5.18
.

As
\mathbb{N}
is a monoid, we could not have used
3.11
. However, the group completion of
\mathbb{N}
is given by
\mathbb{Z}
and so we can ask ourselves how the previous two results compare to the computation of
\mathrm{biHH}(B\mathbb{Z})
and
\mathrm{biHH}(B(\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}))
.

First of all the free loop space of
B\mathbb{Z}
is equivalent to
\mathbb{Z}\times B\mathbb{Z}
, which has an inclusion
\{0\}\coprod_{n\in\{1,2,3,...\}}\{n\}\times B\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}\times B\mathbb{Z}
.

On the other hand the free loop space on
B(\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z})
is given by the groupoid
\mathrm{Fun}(B\mathbb{Z},B(\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}))
, which has objects automorphisms of
B(\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z})
i.e.
\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z}
and morphisms natural transformations, which are given by conjugation. Isomorphism classes of objects are given by conjugacy classes, which correspond to
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
and automorphism group is given by the centralizer, which in case of
(0,0)
is given by
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}
and for any other object is given by
\mathbb{Z}
. Again we have an inclusion

\displaystyle\{(0,0)\}\coprod_{(n,m)\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\backslash\{(0,0)\}}\{(n,m)\}\times B\mathbb{Z}\to B(\mathbb{Z}*\mathbb{Z})\coprod_{(n,m)\in\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}\backslash\{(0,0)\}}B\mathbb{Z}.

Notice, neither inclusion are full, making the result non-trivial and in particular challenging to deduce
\mathrm{biHH}(B\mathbb{N})
from
\mathrm{biHH}(B\mathbb{Z})
.

We now move on to a more complicated example. Let
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+}
be the category of finite ordinals and order-preserving morphisms, which can also be characterized as the category
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
together with one additional initial object corresponding to the empty ordinal. Notice
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+}
is a strictly monoidal category with monoidal structure given by disjoint union and unit given by the empty set.
We want to compute
\mathrm{biHH}(B{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+})
. This requires us to review the paracyclic category, as defined in
[
DK15
, Example I.22]

Definition 5.19
.

Let
\Lambda_{\infty}
be the paracyclic category, with objects
\{0,1,2,...\}
and morphisms

n\to m
given by linear functions
f:\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{Z}
such that
f(l+m+1)=f(l)+n+1
.

The paracyclic category was introduced in
[
FL91
]
, but the name was introduced in
[
GJ93
]
. We need the following concrete characterization of the category as described in
[
DK15
, Example I.28]
.

Remark 5.20
.

The paracyclic category can be characterized via the following generators and relations:

•

Objects
\{0,1,2,...\}

•

Generating morphisms
d^{i}
,
s^{i}

t^{i}

•

s^{i}
and
d^{i}
satisfy the cosimplicial relations and additionally we have for
n\geq 1

–

t^{n}d^{i}=d^{i-1}t^{n-1}
where
i>0

–

t^{n}d^{0}=d^{n}

–

t^{n}s^{i}=s^{i-1}t^{n+1}
, where
i>0

–

t^{n}s^{0}=s^{n}(t^{n+1})^{2}

Theorem 5.21
.

\mathrm{biHH}(B{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+})\simeq(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd}.

Proof.

It suffices to prove that
\mathrm{biHH}(B{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+})
has the same presentation as
(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd}
described in
5.20
. By
5.15
we know that
\mathrm{biHH}(B{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+})
has objects
\mathbb{N}
. The monoidal structure is the same as for
B\mathbb{N}
and so, following
5.16
, there is a unique isomorphism
t^{n}:n\to n
for every
n>0
.

By
5.15
the morphisms are generated by
d^{i},s^{i},t^{i}
, where the interaction of
s^{i},d^{i}
is given by the cosimplicial relations. What remains is to check how the
s^{i},d^{i}
interact with the
t^{i}
.

We start with
s^{0}:[n+1]\to[n]
. We have

t^{n}s^{0}=([n-1],[0])\circ s^{0}=s^{0}([n-1],[1])=s^{0}([n],[0])\circ([n],[0])=s^{0}(t^{n+1})^{2}

Now, let
s^{i}:[n+1]\to[n]
, where
i>0
. Then
s^{i}=\mathrm{id}_{[0]}\coprod s^{i-1}:[0]\coprod[n-1]\to[0]\coprod[n]
. Then we have

t^{n}s^{i}=([n-1],[0])\circ(\mathrm{id}_{[0]}\coprod s^{i-1})=(\mathrm{id}_{[0]}\coprod s^{i-1}([n],[0])=s^{i-1}t^{n+1}

We move on to
d^{i}
. Let
i_{0}:\emptyset\to[0]
be the unique map. Then
d^{0}=i_{0}\coprod\mathrm{id}_{[n-1]}:[n-1]\to[n]
. Hence

t^{n}d^{0}=([n-1],[0])\circ i_{0}\coprod\mathrm{id}_{[n-1]}=\mathrm{id}_{[n-1]}\coprod i_{0}([n],\emptyset)=d^{n}.

Next, for
i>0
,
d^{i}=\mathrm{id}_{[0]}\coprod d^{i-1}:[0]\coprod[n-1]\to[0]\coprod[n]
and so

t^{n}d^{i}=([n-1],[0])\mathrm{id}_{[0]}\coprod d^{i-1}=d^{i-1}\coprod\mathrm{id}_{[0]}([n-2],[0])=d^{i-1}t^{n-1}.

Finally we need to confirm that
\emptyset
is still the initial object and we have

t^{0}i_{0}=(\emptyset,[0])i_{0}=i_{0}(\emptyset,\emptyset)=i_{0},

which proves that
\emptyset
is still initial. This confirms all relations and hence we are done.
∎

We can now use this result to get to the main result of interest, which again requires us to review some concepts regarding the free adjunction category
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
.

Remark 5.22
.

The
free adjunction bicategory

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
can be described as follows. It has two objects
0,1
, and the following morphisms:

•

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(0,0)={\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+}
, where we think of
[n]
as the morphism
(GF)^{n}
.

•

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(1,1)=({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+})^{op}
, the elements of which we denote by
[n]^{op}
to distinguish them from the previous item and think of as
(FG)^{n}
.

•

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(0,1)={\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{max}
, the wide subcategory of
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
consisting of morphisms in
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
that preserve the maximum, the elements of which we denote by
[n]_{max}
and think of as
(FG)^{n}F

•

\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}(0,1)={\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{min}
, the wide subcategory of
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
consisting of morphisms in
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
that preserve the minimum, with elements denoted
[n]_{min}
and think of as
(GF)^{n}G
.

For more details, see the original description of the free adjunction in
[
SS86
]
.

Notice the full subcategory of
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
consisting of
0
is the free monad
2
-category and denoted by
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{on}
, whereas the full subcategory of
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
with object
1
is the free comonad
2
-category and denoted
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{o}\mathscr{M}\mathrm{on}
. We now have the following result.

Theorem 5.23
.

There is a diagram of equivalences of categories
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{on})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{C}\mathrm{o}\mathscr{M}\mathrm{on})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd\rhd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Lambda_{\infty}^{op})^{\rhd}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\simeq}

Proof.

The two equivalences on the left and right side already follows from
5.21
. Hence we will prove the middle map is an equivalence and the diagrams commute.

Following
5.10
and the explicit description of the
2
-category
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}
given above in
5.22
, the category
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})
permits the following general description:

•

Objects two copies of
\mathbb{N}
, which we denote
\emptyset,[0],[1],\cdots
and
\emptyset^{op},[0]^{op},[1]^{op},\cdots
.

•

Morphisms
\sigma:[m]\to[n]
,
\sigma^{op}:[n]^{op}\to[m]^{op}
for all
\sigma\in{\mathbbe{\Delta}}([m],[n])
.

•

Isomorphisms
([n],[m]):[n+m+1]\to[n+m+1]
,
([n]^{op},[m]^{op}):[n+m+1]^{op}\to[n+m+1]^{op}
for objects
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Isomorphisms

([n]_{min},[m]_{max}):[n+m+1]\to[n+m+1]^{op}
and

([m]_{max},[n]_{min}):[n+m+1]^{op}\to[n+m+1]
, where
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
.

•

Relations between the morphisms described in
5.10
.

We will now use the various relations to reduce the structure and obtain the desired result.

First of all, if we restrict to the objects
[0],[1],\cdots
, then we precisely have the generating morphisms and relations of
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+}
and hence, by
5.21
, obtain a copy of
(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd}
. Similarly, the generating morphisms and isomorphisms restricted to
[0]^{op},[1]^{op},\cdots
gives us a copy of
(\Lambda_{\infty}^{op})^{\rhd}
. What remains is to explain how the additional isomorphisms
([n]_{min},[m]_{max})
and
([m]_{max},[n]_{min})
influence
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})
.

Now, for a given
n,m\in\mathbb{N}
, we, by the last relation in
5.10
, have

\displaystyle([n]_{min},[m]_{max})

\displaystyle=([n]_{min},[m-1]_{max}[0]^{op})

\displaystyle=([0]^{op}[n]_{min},[m-1]_{max})\circ([n+m],[0])

\displaystyle=([n+1]_{min},[m-1]_{max})\circ([n+m],[0])

and so, by induction, we we have the equality

([n]_{min},[m]_{max})=([n+m]_{min},[0]_{max})\circ([n+m],[0])^{m}

and similarly

([n]_{max},[m]_{min})=([n+m],[0])^{m}\circ([n+m]_{max},[0]_{min})

Hence, for every
n\in\mathbb{N}
we can reduce the additional isomorphisms of the form
([n]_{min},[0]_{max}):[n+1]\to[n+1]^{op}
and
([n]_{max},[0]_{min}):[n+1]^{op}\to[n+1]
.

Finally, again by the last relation in
5.10
, we have

([n],[0])^{2}=([n-1],[1])=([n]_{max},[0]_{min})\circ([n]_{min},[0]_{max}).

Hence, we can give the following simplified explicit description of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})
:

•

Objects
[0],[1],...
and
[0]^{op},[1]^{op},...

•

Generating morphisms

–

d^{i}:[n]\to[n+1],s^{i}:[n]\to[n-1],t^{n}:[n]\to[n]

–

(d^{i})^{op}:[n]^{op}\to[n+1]^{op},(s^{i})^{op}:[n]^{op}\to[n-1]^{op},(t^{n})^{op}:[n]^{op}\to[n]^{op}

–

c^{n}:[n]\to[n]^{op}

•

Subject to relations

–

d^{i},s^{i},t^{n}
satisfy the paracyclic relations (
5.20
)

–

(d^{i})^{op},(s^{i})^{op},(t^{n})^{op}
satisfy the opposite paracyclic relations

–

c^{n}t^{n}=(t^{n})^{op}c^{n}
as

([n]_{min},[0]_{max})\circ([n],[0])=([n]_{min},[1]_{max})=([n]^{op},[0]^{op})\circ([n]_{min},[0]_{max})

–

c^{n}d^{i}=(d^{i})^{op}c^{n}
, which follows directly from the fourth relation in
5.10
,

–

c^{n}s^{i}=(d^{i})^{op}c^{n}
.

The description of
\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})
as two copies of
\Lambda_{\infty}
and
(\Lambda_{\infty})^{op}
and an additional isomorphism
c_{n}
is just an explicit description of the following pseudo-pushout:
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda_{\infty}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((\Lambda_{\infty})^{op})^{\rhd}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})}

which is equivalent to
(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd\rhd}
and by construction the diagrams above commute.
∎

Remark 5.24
.

In recent work Ayala and Francis have (independently) computed
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})

[
AF21
]
using methods from factorization homology
[
AFR17
]
.

Having this computation we can now deduce the following canonical strengthening of
4.9
.

Corollary 5.25
.

The functor
\widehat{\chi}
is fact given by restricting to the unique map from the initial to terminal object in
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj})\simeq(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd\rhd}
. This means the definition of the Euler characteristic is independent of any choice and is canonical.

We now move on to the final computation.

Theorem 5.26
.

\displaystyle\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})\simeq(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd\rhd}\times\{0\}\coprod_{n\in\{1,2,3,...\}}B\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+}\times\{n\}

Proof.

We want to use the various results in
Section

5
to characterize
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
. We first start by reducing the set of objects so that no two objects are isomorphic.
Following
4.14
and
5.10
the objects in
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
are bijective to

\displaystyle\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(0,0)}\coprod\mathrm{Obj}_{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(1,1)}

\displaystyle\cong\mathbb{N}\ast\mathbb{N}\coprod\mathbb{N}\ast\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle=\{\emptyset,[0],...\}\ast\{0,1,...\}\coprod\{\emptyset^{op},[0]^{op},...\}\ast\{0,1,...\},

where we are using
5.22
.

Now, if
x,y\in\{\emptyset,[0],...\}\ast\{0,1,...\}
, then
5.10
implies that we have an isomorphism
(x,y):xy\to yx
. This means the isomorphism classes are given by the commutators classes of the free words (as already explained in
5.17
). Hence isomorphism classes of objects of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
can in our first step be reduced to

\displaystyle\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\coprod\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle=\{\emptyset,[0],...\}\times\{0,1,...\}\coprod\{\emptyset^{op},[0]^{op},...\}\times\{0,1,...\}

\displaystyle\cong\{[n]k,[n]^{op}k:[n]\in\mathrm{Obj}_{{\mathbbe{\Delta}}},k\in\mathbb{N}\}

We will make one further reduction of our objects.
Indeed, using the same argument as in
5.23
(and again notation
5.22
) it follows that

([n-1]_{min},[0]_{max})k:[n]k\to[n]^{op}k

is an isomorphism. Hence, we can conclude that the isomorphism classes of objects are precisely given by

\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\cong\{[n]k:[n]\in\mathrm{Obj}_{{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+}},k\in\mathbb{N}\}.

Now that we have determined the objects, we can move on to precisely the determine the generating morphisms and isomorphisms, again using
5.10
.

By the explanation in
4.14
, morphisms in
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(0,0)
are given by words of morphisms in
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+}
and so there is only a morphism from
[n_{1}]m_{1}
to
[n_{2}]m_{2}
if and only if
m_{1}=m_{2}
. Hence

(5.27)

\mathrm{Mor}_{\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(0,0)}=\{\sigma k:[n]k\to[m]k:\sigma\in{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+}([n],[m]),k\in\mathbb{N}\}

On the other side, the monoidal structure on
\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd}(0,0)
coincides with the one on
B(\mathbb{N}\ast\mathbb{N})
and so, as explained in
5.17
, every object
[n]k
has a unique generating non-trivial automorphism i.e.
\mathrm{Aut}_{\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})}([n]k)=\mathbb{Z}
.

In order to finish the proof we need to understand the interaction between the automorphisms and morphisms, using the relations given in
5.10
. Let
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})_{k}
be the full subcategory of
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
consisting of objects of the form
[n]k
. The explanation in
5.27
implies that

\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})\cong\coprod_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})_{k}

Hence, we can break down our analysis into the different
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})_{k}
.

Let us start with
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})_{0}
. In that case we have morphisms
\sigma 0:[n]0\to[m]0
, for
\sigma:[n]\to[m]
in
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+}
and automorphisms
t^{n}:[n]0\to[n]0
, which, by the relations given in
5.23
interact precisely as given in
5.23
and so we have the equivalence

\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})\simeq(\Lambda_{\infty})^{\lhd\rhd}.

Now, let us assume
k>0
. Then the generating isomorphism of
[n]k
is given by the symbol
(\emptyset 0,[n]k)
, which, by
5.10
, satisfies the equality

((\emptyset 0,[n]k)=([n]0,\emptyset k)\circ(\emptyset k,[n]0),

where
([n]0,\emptyset k):[n]k\to k[n]
is a twisting isomorphism and
(\emptyset k,[n]0)
is defined similarly.
Now, for an arbitrary morphism
\sigma k:[n]k\to[m]k
in
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})_{k}
, where
\sigma:[n]\to[m]
, we have

\displaystyle((\emptyset 0,[m]k)\circ\sigma k

\displaystyle=([m]0,\emptyset k)\circ(\emptyset k,[m]0)\circ\sigma

\displaystyle=([m]0,\emptyset k)\circ k\sigma\circ(\emptyset k,[n]0)

\displaystyle=\sigma k\circ([n]0,\emptyset k)\circ(\emptyset k,[n]0)

\displaystyle=\sigma k\circ(\emptyset 0,[n]k)

Here, for the third equality we used the fact that
m\neq 0
. This shows that
(\emptyset 0,[n]k)
commutes will all morphism, proving the desired equivalence

\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})_{m}\simeq B\mathbb{Z}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{+},

finishing the proof.
∎

Now combining this computation with
4.16
we obtain the following key observation.

Corollary 5.28
.

The functor
\widehat{\mathrm{tr}}
is obtained by restricting to the morphism
\emptyset 1\to[0]1
in
\mathrm{biHH}(\mathscr{A}\mathrm{dj}\mathscr{E}\mathrm{nd})
. As
\mathrm{Aut}(\emptyset 1)=\mathbb{Z}
, this means
\widehat{\mathrm{tr}}
is in fact not canonically determined and is only fixed up to a choice of automorphism.

Notice the non-canonicity does not influence the result in
4.17
, however it is not clear whether the choice of automorphism would influence other future results.

6.
Towards shadows on
(\infty,2)
-categories

We are finally in a position to generalize shadows to
(\infty,2)
-categories. To motivate our definition, we first analyze the nature of shadows on the homotopy bicategory of a
(\infty,2)
-category with values in homotopy category of an
(\infty,1)
-category.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be an
(\infty,2)
-category specified by a symmetric monoidal functor

\mathscr{B}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{\mathscr{B}_{0}}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}.

Its
homotopy bicategory

\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{B})
is the bicategory specified by the composite

\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{\mathscr{B}_{0}}\xrightarrow{\mathscr{B}}\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}_{\infty}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{Ho}}\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}.

This composite does indeed give rise to a bicategory (i.e., enriched over
\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
in the sense of
2.8
), since
\mathrm{Ho}
preserves finite products and thus is symmetric monoidal.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be an
(\infty,2)
-category and
\mathscr{D}
an
(\infty,1)
-category. There is an equivalence

\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}\big(\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{B}),\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{D})\big)\simeq\mathrm{Fun}_{\infty}\big(\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{B}),\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{D})\big),

where on the right-hand side we implicitly view
\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{D})
as an
(\infty,1)
-category via the nerve functor.

Proof.

There are equivalences

\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}\big(\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{B}),\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{D})\big)\simeq\mathrm{Fun}\big(\mathrm{biHH}(\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{B})),\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{D})\big)\simeq\mathrm{Fun}_{\infty}\big(\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{B}),\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{D})\big).

The first equivalence is an immediate consequence of
3.19
.
The second follows from the fact that
\mathrm{Ho}
commutes with colimits and so

\mathrm{biHH}(\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{B}))\simeq\mathrm{Ho}(\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{B})),

together with the fact that
\mathrm{Ho}
is left adjoint to the nerve functor (
Section

1.6
).
∎

In particular, there is a localization functor

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{B}),\mathscr{D})\to\mathscr{S}\mathrm{ha}\big(\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{B}),\mathrm{Ho}(\mathscr{D})\big)

that takes a trace of
(\infty,2)
-categories to a shadow.

Motivated by
3.19
and the analysis above, we define a
shadow
on an
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathscr{B}
with values in an
(\infty,1)
-category
\mathscr{D}
to be a trace (
2.17
), i.e., an
\infty
-functor

\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{B})\to\mathscr{D},

where
\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{B})
is constructed as in
2.10
.
This definition can be viewed as a homotopy coherent lift of the definition of a shadow.

It is natural to ask when
(\infty,2)
-categorical shadows exist and how to construct them. In the important special case of
\mathscr{V}
-enriched categories and their bimodules, we conjecture that there should an exist an analogue of the Hochschild shadow for spectral categories.

Conjecture 6.2
.

Let
(\mathscr{V},\otimes,I)
be a presentably symmetric monoidal
(\infty,1)
-category. Let
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}}
be the
(\infty,2)
-category with as objects
\mathscr{V}
-enriched categories and morphisms given by bimodules (cf.
[
Hau16
]
). There exists a functor of
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}:\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}})\to\mathscr{V}.

Moreover, if
\mathscr{V}
is closed symmetric monoidal, then
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}
is a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched functor of
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories

We cannot yet prove this conjecture, but take an important first step and construct a functor to
\mathrm{Ho}\mathscr{V}
(
6.11
), then discuss some technical challenges that arise when trying to lift to an
(\infty,2)
-categorical shadow (
6.14
). We begin by relativizing Berman’s approach to
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories
[
Ber22
]
in order to describe
\mathscr{V}
-enriched bimodules.

Definition 6.3
.

Let
S,T
be two sets. A
directed graph from
S
to
T
is a directed graph with vertex set
S\coprod T
, set of edges
E
, and source and target maps
s,t:E\to S\coprod T
such that if
t(e)\in S
, then
s(e)\in S
, i.e., there are no edges starting in
T
and ending in
S

Definition 6.4
.

Let
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}
be the full subcategory of
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\coprod T}
with objects the directed graphs from
S
to
T
.

This full subcategory is still symmetric monoidal, as graphs from
S
to
T
are closed under disjoint union, and the empty graph on
S\coprod T
is in particular a graph from
S
to
T
.

The following notation for objects in
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}
proves useful below.

Notation 6.5
.

Let
X,Y\in S\coprod T
, where if
Y\in S
, then
X\in S
. There exists a graph with a unique edge from
X
to
Y
, which we denote
(X,Y)
. More generally, let
(X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n})
be the graph with a single path
X_{0}\to X_{1}\to...\to X_{n}
, where if
X_{i}\in S
, then
X_{j}\in S
for all
j<i
.

As
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}
is defined as a full symmetric monoidal subcategory of
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\coprod T}
, the explanation after
[
Ber22
, Definition 2.3]
provides an explicit description of the symmetric monoidal category via generators and relations as follows.

•

Objects are pairs
(X,Y)
of elements of
S\coprod T
, where if
Y\in S
, then
X\in S
.

•

Morphisms are generated by
(X,Y,Z)=(X,Y)\otimes(Y,Z)\to(X,Z)
, which exists for every triple
X,Y,Z\in S\coprod T
, where
Z\in S
implies
X,Y\in S
, and
Y\in S
implies
X\in S
.

•

For every
X\in S\coprod T
, there is an identity morphism
\emptyset\to(X,X)

•

The associativity and unitality relations of
[
Ber22
, Definition 2.3]
.

There is an evident inclusion functor
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}\coprod\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T}\to\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}
. We can use this to define our desired modules.

Definition 6.6
.

Let
\mathscr{C}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}\to\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{D}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T}\to\mathscr{V}
be two
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories. A
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
-bimodule
is a symmetric monoidal functor
\mathscr{M}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}\to\mathscr{V}
that makes the following diagram commute.
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{V}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{C}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{D}}

It is helpful to unpack this definition somewhat. The objects in
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}
can be classified into three distinct types: graphs whose edges all start and end at elements of
S
, graphs whose edges all start and end at elements of
T
, and graphs that have an edge that starts in
S
and ends in
T
. The first two types of graph lie in the essential image of the inclusion functor
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}\coprod\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T}\to\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}
, and so the value of
\mathscr{M}
on those graphs is predetermined. In particular for every
X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S
,

\mathscr{M}(X_{0},...,X_{n})=\mathscr{C}(X_{0},...,X_{n}),

and for every
X_{0},...,X_{n}\in T
,

\mathscr{M}(X_{0},...,X_{n})=\mathscr{D}(X_{0},...,X_{n}).

On the other hand if
X_{0}\in S
and
X_{1}\in T
, then there are no constraints on the object
\mathscr{M}(X_{0},X_{1})
in
\mathscr{V}
.

In
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}
, for every
X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S
and
X_{n+1}\in T
, there a bypass morphism
(X_{0},...,X_{n+1})\to(X_{0},X_{n+1})
, the image of which under
\mathscr{M}
is a map

\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{n-1},X_{n})\otimes\mathscr{M}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\to\mathscr{M}(X_{0},X_{n+1}).

Similarly for
X_{0}\in S
and
X_{1},...,X_{n+1}\in T
, there is a map

\mathscr{M}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes\mathscr{D}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{D}(X_{n},X_{n+1})\to\mathscr{M}(X_{0},X_{n+1}).

This is precisely the expected structural data of a bimodule. The composition rules in
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}
guarantees that these bimodule actions satisfy the appropriate coherence conditions.

Before we proceed, it is important to confirm that the definition given here matches with the existing literature.
Bimodules of
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories have been studied extensively by Haugseng
[
Hau16
]
. We can confirm that the definition above does indeed coincide with that of Haugseng, by a slight generalization of the argument given by Berman in
[
Ber22
, Proposition 2.7]
.

Proposition 6.7
.

The notion of bimodules formulated in
6.6
coincides with that of Haugseng
[
Hau16
, Definition 4.3]
.

Proof.

We follow the lines of the proof of
[
Ber22
, Proposition 2.7]
. First recall that a bimodule is an algebra on the non-symmetric
\infty
-operad
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}_{S,T}
(described explicitly in
[
Hau16
, Definition 4.1]
). Because
\mathscr{V}
is symmetric monoidal, we can use the symmetric monoidal envelope of
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}_{S,T}
which is given by the active morphisms
[
Lur17
, Construction 2.2.4.1]
.

As in the proof of
[
Ber22
, Proposition 2.7]
, the active morphisms to a pair
(A,B)
of elements of
S\coprod T
are of the form

(X_{1},Y_{1})\otimes...\otimes(X_{n},Y_{n})\to(A,B).

It follows that the symmetric monoidal envelope of
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{S,T}^{op}
is given by
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}
and the result then follows by the universal property of symmetric monoidal envelopes
[
Lur17
, Proposition 2.2.4.9]
.
∎

We are now almost ready to define
\mathrm{THH}
for
\infty
-categorical bimodules. This requires one last definition.

Definition 6.8
.

For
S,T
two sets, define the symmetric monoidal category
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft T}
as the pushout of symmetric monoidal categories
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}\coprod\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T,S}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft T}}

Now, let
\mathscr{C}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}\to\mathscr{V}
be a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category and
\mathscr{M}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,S}\to\mathscr{V}
a
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
-bimodule. To distinguish between the two copies of
S
, we denote an element
X\in S
as
X^{\prime}
if it is in the second copy. By universal property of pushouts,
\mathscr{M}
lifts to a functors
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft S}\to\mathscr{V}
, which we also denote by
\mathscr{M}
, to simplify notation.

Let
\mathscr{M}^{*}:\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft S})\to\mathscr{V}
be the left Kan extension of
\mathscr{M}
. Let
(\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}})_{\bullet}:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft S})
be the functor defined by

(\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}})_{n}=\coprod_{X_{0},...,,X_{n}\in S}(X_{0},X_{1},...,X_{n},X_{0}^{\prime}),

where we are using
6.5
.

Definition 6.9
.

For any
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-category
\mathscr{C}
and
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
-bimodule
\mathscr{M}
, the
\mathscr{V}
-enriched Hochschild homology of
\mathscr{C}
with coefficients in
\mathscr{M}
, denoted
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{M})
, is the object in
\mathscr{V}
that is the colimit of
\mathscr{M}^{*}\circ(\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}})_{\bullet}
.

Since
\mathscr{M}^{*}
preserves colimits,
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{M})
is equivalent to the colimit of the simplicial diagram

\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}}\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{C}_{(}X_{n-1},X_{n})\otimes\mathscr{M}(X_{n},X_{0})

Our goal is to show that the collection of the
\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{M})
underlies a shadow. To do so, we need an appropriate bicategory. We begin by defining morphisms of bimodules.

Definition 6.10
.

Let
\mathscr{C}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}\to\mathscr{V}
and
\mathscr{D}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T}\to\mathscr{V}
be
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories, and let
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{N}
be
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
-bimodules. A
morphism of bimodules
is a natural transformation
\alpha:\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{N}
such that
\alpha
restricts to the identity on
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}
and
\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T}
.

In
[
Hau16
, Theorem 1.2]
Haugseng proves that there is an
(\infty,2)
-category
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}}
with

•

\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories as objects,

•

bimodules as morphisms,

•

morphisms of bimodules as
2
-morphisms.

We are now ready to generalize
3.29
.

Theorem 6.11
.

There is a functor of
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}:\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}})\to\mathrm{Ho}\mathscr{V}

such that that the corresponding shadow on
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}}
(cf.
6.1
) sends any
\mathscr{C}
-bimodule
\mathscr{M}
to
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{M})
.

Proof.

We first need to show that the construction of
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{M})
is natural in the coefficient bimodule, i.e., that it extends to a functor
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},-):\mathrm{Ho}\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})\to\mathrm{Ho}\mathscr{V}
for every
\mathscr{V}
-enriched category
\mathscr{C}
. For any
\mathscr{C}
-bimodule morphism
\alpha:\mathscr{M}\to\mathscr{N}
, we define
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},\alpha)
by

\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},\alpha)=\mathrm{colim}_{{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}}\alpha^{*}\circ\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}}:\mathrm{colim}_{{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}}\mathscr{M}^{*}\circ\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}}\to\mathrm{colim}_{{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}}\mathscr{N}^{*}\circ\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}}.

This construction is functorial, as it can be described as the following composite of functors.

\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft S},\mathscr{V})\xrightarrow{Lan}\mathrm{Fun}\big(\mathrm{Fun}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft S}^{op},\mathscr{S}),\mathscr{V}\big)\xrightarrow{(\mathscr{O}_{hh_{\mathscr{V}}})^{*}}\mathrm{Fun}({\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op},\mathscr{V})\xrightarrow{\mathrm{colim}}\mathscr{V}

It remains to define the twisting isomorphism for this shadow. To do so, we adapt the Dennis-Morita-Waldhausen argument
[
BM12
, Proposition 6.2]
, which is also used by Campbell and Ponto
[
CP19
, Theorem 2.17]
, to the
\infty
-categorical setting.

The first step towards defining the twisting isomorphism consists of the following bisimplicial construction. Let
(\mathscr{O}^{S\circlearrowleft T}_{thh})_{\bullet\bullet}:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft T})
be the functor that takes
([n],[m])
to

\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S,Y_{0},...,Y_{m}\in T}(X_{0},...,X_{n},Y_{0},...,Y_{m},X_{0}),

and let
\mathscr{O}^{S\circlearrowleft T}_{thh}
be the colimit of this bisimplicial diagram in
\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft T})
.
Similarly, define
(\mathscr{O}^{T\circlearrowleft S}_{thh})_{\bullet\bullet}:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft T})
as the functor with value

\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S,Y_{0},...,Y_{m}\in T}(Y_{0},...,Y_{m},X_{0},...,X_{n},Y_{0}),

on
([n],[m])
and
\mathscr{O}^{T\circlearrowleft S}_{thh}
as its colimit in
\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft T})
.

The symmetric monoidal structure of the bypass categories provides us with a canonical isomorphism

(X_{0},...,X_{n},Y_{0},...,Y_{m},X_{0})\overset{\cong}{\longrightarrow}(Y_{0},...,Y_{m},X_{0},...,X_{n},Y_{0}),

which implies that there is an equivalence

(6.12)

\mathscr{O}^{S\circlearrowleft T}_{thh}\overset{\cong}{\Longrightarrow}\mathscr{O}^{T\circlearrowleft S}_{thh}.

in
\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft T})
.

We are now ready to define the desired twisting isomorphism. Let

\mathscr{C}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S}\to\mathscr{V}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathscr{D}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T}\to\mathscr{V}

be two
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories, and let
\mathscr{M}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S,T}\to\mathscr{V}
be a
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{D})
-bimodule and
\mathscr{N}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{T,S}\to\mathscr{V}
a
(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{C})
-bimodule.
The universal property of the pushout implies that these data give rise to functor

\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N}:\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft T}\to\mathscr{V},

which we can left Kan extend to a functor

(\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N})^{*}:\mathcal{P}(\mathscr{B}\mathrm{ypass}_{S\circlearrowleft T}^{op})\to\mathscr{V}.

Note that
(\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N})^{*}(\mathscr{O}^{S\circlearrowleft T}_{thh})
is the colimit of the bisimplicial object

(\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N})^{*}\circ(\mathscr{O}^{S\circlearrowleft T}_{thh})_{\bullet\bullet}:{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\times{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}\to\mathscr{V}

that takes
([n],[m])
to

\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S,Y_{0},...,Y_{m}\in T}\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{n-1},X_{n})\otimes\mathscr{M}(X_{n},Y_{0})\otimes\mathscr{D}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{D}(Y_{m-1},Y_{m})\otimes\mathscr{N}(Y_{m},X_{0}).

Fix an object
[n]
in
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
. By
[
Hau16
, End of Section 5]
, the colimit of the simplicial diagram

\displaystyle(\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N})^{*}\circ(\mathscr{O}^{S\circlearrowleft T}_{thh})_{n\bullet}=

\displaystyle\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S,Y_{0},...,Y_{\bullet}\in T}\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{n-1},X_{n})\otimes\mathscr{M}(X_{n},Y_{0})\otimes\mathscr{D}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{N}(Y_{\bullet},X_{0})

is

\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S}\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{n-1},X_{n})\otimes(\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N})(X_{n},X_{0}),

where
\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N}
is the
(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{C})
-bimodule obtained via tensor product of
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{N}
as defined in
[
Hau16
, Remark 5.4]
.
By
6.9
the colimit of this simplicial object is precisely
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N})
.

Repeating the same argument with the roles of
\mathscr{M}
and
\mathscr{N}
reversed, we deduce that
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{N}\otimes\mathscr{M})
can be obtained as the colimit of the bisimplicial diagram of the form

\coprod_{X_{0},...,X_{n}\in S,Y_{0},...,Y_{m}\in T}\mathscr{D}(Y_{0},Y_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{D}(Y_{n-1},Y_{m})\otimes\mathscr{N}(Y_{m},X_{0})\otimes\mathscr{C}(X_{0},X_{1})\otimes...\otimes\mathscr{M}(X_{n},Y_{0}),

i.e.,
(\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N})^{*}(\mathscr{O}^{T\circlearrowleft S}_{thh})
. Equivalence (
6.12
) then implies that

\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{D},\mathscr{N}\otimes\mathscr{M})\simeq\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{M}\otimes\mathscr{N}),

as desired.

Finally, as mentioned in the proof of
[
CP19
, Theorem 2.17]
, it is straightforward to check that this isomorphism satisfies the two compatibility conditions of
2.3
, and hence we can conclude.
∎

We can apply
6.11
to the case of spectrally enriched
\infty
-categories and thus, in particular, to stable
\infty
-categories, since every stable
\infty
-category is in fact enriched over
\mathrm{Sp}

[
GH15
, Example 7.4.14]
.

Corollary 6.13
.

There is a functor of
\infty
-categories

\mathrm{THH}:\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathrm{Sp}})\to\mathrm{Ho}\mathrm{Sp}

such that that the corresponding shadow on
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathrm{Sp}}
(cf.
6.1
) sends any spectral bimodule
\mathscr{M}
to
\mathrm{THH}(\mathscr{C},\mathscr{M})
.

We end this section with a discussion of possible obstructions to further generalizations.

Remark 6.14
.

To establish
6.2
, we need to prove that if
\mathscr{V}
is closed monoidal (i.e., if
\mathscr{V}=\mathrm{Sp}
), then the functor
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}:\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}})\to\mathscr{V}
is a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched functor of
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories. While it is clear that
\mathscr{V}
is
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
[
GH15
, Corollary 7.4.10]
, proving that
\mathrm{biHH}_{\infty}(\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}})
is
\mathscr{V}
-enriched requires showing that
\mathscr{M}\mathrm{od}_{\mathscr{V}}
is a
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
(\infty,2)
-category i.e., enriched in
\mathscr{V}
-enriched
\infty
-categories rather than just
\infty
-categories.

If
\mathscr{V}
is presentable, then this is expected to be true and should be established in future work
3
3

3

Based on private conversation with Rune Haugseng.
.The existing literature, such as
[
Hau16
]
, does not include such results, however, and makes the further study of
\mathrm{HH}_{\mathscr{V}}
more challenging.

Appendix A
Truncated Simplicial Pseudo-Diagrams

In this appendix we perform a detailed analysis of pseudofunctors out of the truncated simplex category
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
, which is essential for our proof of
3.19
.

A
2
-category is by definition a category enriched over categories. Hence, there is a default notion of morphisms between
2
-categories, called
2
-functors
, which are strict functors that respect the categorical enrichment. On the other hand, we can also consider
pseudofunctors
, for which the composition and unit hold only up to chosen natural isomorphisms. For a more detailed discussion of pseudofunctors see
[
Gro71
,
Bén67
]
.

We want to characterize pseudofunctors out of
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
and out of
(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op})^{\rhd}
into a
2
-category
\mathscr{B}
. Constructing a pseudofunctor by hand can be quite challenging, however, since we need to specify a natural isomorphism for every pair of composable morphisms in the domain. Fortunately, there is a way to simplify the task. In
[
Lac02
,
Lac04
]
Lack constructs a model structure on the category of
2
-categories that has the property that any pseudofunctor between
2
-categories with cofibrant domain is equivalent to a
2
-functor
[
Lac02
, Remark 4.10]
.

We start therefore by recalling the characterization of the cofibrant objects in Lack’s model structure, which requires the notion of
free categories
. Recall that a graph is a specified by a pair of functions with the same domain and the same codomain. Every small category has an
underlying graph
, given by the source and target functions on the set of morphisms, i.e., there is a forgetful functor
U:\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}\to\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rph}
. The forgetful functor admits a left adjoint
\mathscr{F}_{1}:\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rph}\to\mathscr{C}\mathrm{at}
, which takes a graph to its
free category
. For more details regarding free categories and graphs see
[
ML98
, Section II.7]
. The following result regarding cofibrant objects summarizes
[
Lac02
, Theorem 4.8]
.

Theorem A.1
.

A
2
-category is cofibrant in Lack’s model structure if and only its underlying
1
-category is a free category on a graph.

Unfortunately, the categories
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
and
(({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op})^{\rhd}
are not cofibrant in Lack’s model structure on
2
-categories. Indeed the generating morphisms satisfy non-trivial relations (cf.
Lemma

A.2
). Hence our first task is to find appropriate cofibrant replacements, for which we apply the theory of
computads
introduced in
[
Str76
]
. A computad consists of a graph
\mathscr{G}
together with a set of
2
-arrows between parallel morphisms in the free category on
\mathscr{G}
. We refer to the original source
[
Str76
, Section 2]
for a more complete description, as we need only certain computads.

Before we proceed, it is helpful to describe fully the category
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
, in particular its morphisms. As it is a full subcategory of
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}^{op}
, we can state the following result in terms of the characterization of
{\mathbbe{\Delta}}
via generators and relations found in
[
GJ09
, Section I.1]
and
[
ML98
, Section VII.5]

Lemma A.2
.

The category
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
can be described as follows.

•

It has three objects:
0,1,2
.

•

It has eight generating morphisms.

–

s_{0}:0\to 1

–

d_{0},d_{1}:1\to 0

–

s_{0},s_{1}:1\to 2

–

d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}:2\to 1

•

The generating morphisms satisfy the following relations.

–

d_{0}d_{0}=d_{0}d_{1},d_{0}d_{2}=d_{1}d_{0},d_{1}d_{1}=d_{1}d_{2}

–

d_{1}s_{0}=\mathrm{id}=d_{0}s_{0}

–

d_{0}s_{0}=d_{1}s_{1}=\mathrm{id}=d_{1}s_{0}=d_{2}s_{1}

–

s_{0}s_{0}=s_{1}s_{0}

Motivated by this description, we now construct the desired computad, starting with its underlying graph.

Definition A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{D}_{2}
be the graph with three objects
\{0,1,2\}
and eight arrows
\{s_{0}:0\to 1;d_{0},d_{1}:1\to 0;s_{0},s_{1}:1\to 2;d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}:2\to 1\}
.

Next we add the relevant
2
-arrows based on the relations in
Lemma

A.2
.

Definition A.4
.

Let
\mathcal{G}_{2}
be the computad with underlying graph
\mathcal{D}_{2}
, equipped with following
2
-arrows in
\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{2})
.

•

d_{0}d_{0}\Rightarrow d_{0}d_{1},d_{0}d_{2}\Rightarrow d_{1}d_{0},d_{1}d_{1}\Rightarrow d_{1}d_{2}

•

d_{1}s_{0}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}\Leftarrow d_{0}s_{0}

•

d_{0}s_{0}\Rightarrow d_{1}s_{1}\Rightarrow\mathrm{id}\Leftarrow d_{1}s_{0}\Leftarrow d_{2}s_{1}

•

s_{0}s_{0}\Rightarrow s_{1}s_{0}

Applying
[
Str76
, Theorem 2]
to the computad
\mathcal{G}_{2}
gives us following result.

Lemma A.5
.

There is a
2
-category
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
satisfying the universal property that every
2
-functor
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})\to\mathscr{B}
is specified by the following data.

•

A functor from the free category
\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{2})
on the graph of
\mathcal{D}_{2}
to the underlying category of
\mathscr{B}
.

•

A choice of
2
-morphism in
\mathscr{B}
with the appropriate source and target for every
2
-arrow of the computad
\mathcal{G}_{2}
.

We refer to
[
Str76
, Page 155]
for a detailed explanation of what “appropriate” means in this context.

Applying the universal property of the free category functor, let

\pi_{1}:\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{2})\to({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}

denote the functor that is the identity on objects and generating
1
-morphisms. Note that by the universal property of
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
, there is a
2
-functor

\pi_{2}:\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{D}_{2})\to({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}

with underlying functor
\pi_{1}
and sending every 2-arrow of the computad
\mathcal{D}_{2}
to an identity.

Before proceeding further, we analyze the structure of the
2
-category
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
and the relations satisfied by its
2
-morphisms.

Lemma A.6
.

The
2
-functor
\pi_{2}:\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})\to({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
is a cofibrant replacement in Lack’s model structure on bicategories.

Proof.

By
A.1
, the
2
-category
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
is evidently cofibrant as its underlying category is just
\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{2})
, which is free by definition.

We therefore need only to show that the projection map

\pi_{2}:\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{D}_{2})\to({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}

is a weak equivalence in Lack’s model structure. However, as follows from the proof of
[
Lac02
, Proposition 4.2]
and the explanation immediately thereafter, this functor is actually a trivial fibration.
∎

We apply this result to establish a useful characterization of the
2
-category
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
. To do so, it is helpful to review
contractible groupoids
.

Lemma A.7
.

The forgetful functor
\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}\to\mathscr{S}\text{et}
, which takes a groupoid to its set of objects, admits a right adjoint
I:\mathscr{S}\text{et}\to\mathscr{G}\mathrm{rpd}
, which takes a set
S
to the groupoid
I(S)
with the same set of objects and a unique isomorphism between any two objects. Moreover, a category with object set
S
is contractible if and only if it is isomorphic to
I(S)
.

Proof.

Only the last sentence requires an argument. If
\mathscr{C}
is a contractible category with object set
S
, then for any object
s\in S
, the functor
s:[0]\to\mathscr{C}
is an equivalence and thus fully faithful, which implies that
\mathscr{C}(s,s)=\{\mathrm{id}_{s}\}
.

Let
s,s^{\prime}\in S
. Since the map
s:[0]\to\mathscr{C}
is an equivalence, it is essentially surjective, whence there exists an isomorphism
s\to s^{\prime}
in
\mathscr{C}
, which must be unique. Indeed, the existence of two distinct isomorphisms
f,g:s\to s^{\prime}
would imply the existence of a non-trivial automorphism
g^{-1}f:s\to s
, in contradiction with the conclusion of the previous paragraph. We conclude that
\mathscr{C}
is isomorphic to
I(S)
, as desired.
∎

We call
I(S)
the
contractible groupoid based on the set
S
, as it is equivalent to the terminal category. We can formulate an alternative characterization of the morphism categories in
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
in terms of contractible groupoids.

Lemma A.8
.

The
2
-category
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
has three objects
0,1,2
. Moreover, for any
i,j\in\{0,1,2\}
, the category
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})(i,j)
has as objects the set
\mathscr{F}_{1}(\mathcal{D}_{2})(i,j)
, while for two objects
f,g
, there is a unique morphism from
f
to
g
if and only and only if
\pi_{1}(f)=\pi_{1}(g)
in
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
. In particular,
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})(i,j)
is a groupoid, and there is an isomorphism of groupoids

\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})(i,j)\cong\coprod_{f\in({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}(i,j)}I\big((\pi_{1})^{-1}(f)\big).

Proof.

The characterization of the underlying category of the
2
-category
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})
follows from
Lemma

A.5
. For all
i,j\in\{0,1,2\}
,
Lemma

A.6
implies that

\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})(i,j)\to({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}(i,j)

is an equivalence of categories. Since the right hand side is discrete,
\mathscr{F}_{2}(\mathcal{G}_{2})(i,j)
is a disjoint union of contractible categories. The desired isomorphism now follows from the characterization of contractible categories given in
Lemma

A.7
.
∎

We next use this cofibrant replacement to characterize pseudofunctors out of
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
. To simplify notation, we henceforth denote the cofibrant replacement above by

\pi_{Q}:Q({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}.

The next result is an immediate consequence of
Lemma

A.8
and
[
Lac02
, Remark 4.10]
.

Corollary A.9
.

Let
\mathscr{B}
be a
2
-category.
Precomposing with the cofibrant replacement functor
\pi_{Q}:Q({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
induces an equivalence between the category of pseudofunctors
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to\mathscr{B}
and that of
2
-functors
Q({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to\mathscr{B}
. In other words, for every pseudofunctor
F:({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to\mathscr{B}
, there exists a
2
-functor
\widehat{F}:Q({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to\mathscr{B}
and an equivalence
F\circ\pi_{Q}\simeq\widehat{F}
.

Combining the results above, we can now characterize pseudofunctors out of
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}
.

Lemma A.10
.

For any
2
-category
\mathscr{B}
, a pseudofunctor
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to\mathscr{B}
is determined up to equivalence by a choice of the following data.

•

Three objects
B_{0},B_{1},B_{2}

•

Eight
1
-morphisms

–

s_{0}:B_{0}\to B_{1}

–

d_{0},d_{1}:B_{1}\to B_{0}

–

s_{0},s_{1}:B_{1}\to B_{2}

–

d_{0},d_{1},d_{2}:B_{2}\to B_{1}

•

Ten invertible
2
-morphisms

–

d_{0}d_{0}\overset{\alpha_{0}}{\Rightarrow}d_{0}d_{1},d_{0}d_{2}\overset{\alpha_{1}}{\Rightarrow}d_{1}d_{0},d_{1}d_{1}\overset{\alpha_{2}}{\Rightarrow}d_{1}d_{2}
in
\mathscr{B}(B_{2},B_{0})

–

d_{1}s_{0}\overset{\beta_{0}}{\Rightarrow}\mathrm{id}\overset{\beta_{1}}{\Leftarrow}d_{0}s_{0}
in
\mathscr{B}(B_{0},B_{0})

–

d_{0}s_{0}\overset{\gamma_{0}}{\Rightarrow}d_{1}s_{1}\overset{\gamma_{1}}{\Rightarrow}\mathrm{id}\overset{\gamma_{2}}{\Leftarrow}d_{1}s_{0}\overset{\gamma_{3}}{\Leftarrow}d_{2}s_{1}
in
\mathscr{B}(B_{1},B_{1})

–

s_{0}s_{0}\overset{\delta_{0}}{\Rightarrow}s_{1}s_{0}
in
\mathscr{B}(B_{0},B_{2})

Moreover, any two
2
-morphisms generated by the
2
-morphisms above with equal domain and codomain are equal.

Proof.

By
A.9
a pseudofunctor
({\mathbbe{\Delta}}_{\leq 2})^{op}\to\mathscr{B}
is determined up to equivalence by a
2
-functor
